# Chapter Summaries

Rolling window: **Volume 01, Chapters 1–50** (Batch 0001, "The First Bell"; Batch 0002, "The Roster"; Batch 0003, "The Tour"; Batch 0004, "The Ledger"; **Batch 0005, "The Memorial"**). **Volume 01 is complete as drafted. Nothing has been archived out of this file.**

---

## Chapter 0001 — Night Round

Tue 19:40 – Wed 03:10. Elias fixes the Hall of Former Faiths door (warped strike plate, thirty years of paint) and logs the time. The River Works notice dated Monday — third ward-bell at South Sluice re-cracked at 21:40, sounding a wrong note — is on the pinboard, and the crew have already taken it as the building's ready-made explanation for any noise this week. At 02:52 plinth 34 gives one note with nobody near it. Elias does not run; he checks the case, the seal, the sensor, the door, finds everything correct, and writes it into the building log as a building-noise observation, exactly as Dael would have it. He thinks about his father's unopened letter and does nothing. Ends with the sound now being a record, and forty-one children booked into that hall on Saturday.

## Chapter 0002 — The Bell in the Hall

Wed 19:00–22:10. Dael reads the log before Elias arrives and gives an instruction, not a question: west range, ductwork, note it and move on; an undocumented danger is a danger he can be dismissed for; leave the sluice notice up because the Board has been on about the ward-bell budget since March. Elias logs properly and keeps a private pocket notebook as a second, unlogged copy — the batch's first real cost, paid in risk. At 20:55 the note recurs with Elias in the hall; the case does nothing, and the hall's background hum from every other object drops out and comes back one second later. The Index surfaces one line, which he does not understand and does not write down. He meets Mara Iles in a first-floor doorway: there is no accession file 4471 over B. Saturday's booking sheet is on the desk under a lead. He writes *not investigated, per instruction*. The chapter closes on ninety minutes of silence and, at 22:10, the conservation treatment rooms going off one floor above him. **The bell does not sound again for the rest of the shift; the third note belongs to Chapter 3.**

## Chapter 0003 — A Number That Does Not Add

Wed 22:10 – Thu 00:30. Mara examines the bell as material evidence and beats him on two counts. The first is arithmetic: a Museum accession is **year / letters / number**, `1961/LW/44`, where the year is the year the district *series* was opened — so `Acq. 4471 / B`, with no year and one letter where the form takes two, is not a Museum number at all, and it is the only one of the label's three claims that anybody typed. The second is wear: the hollow is a left-hand grip, not a rope, so the bell was rung hard for years by somebody who never maintained it. She identifies the repair staple as a decision somebody made about a Tuesday morning, over a crack that is still visible. She also refuses to be told what he heard, and makes him sit through the difference between his word and an entry in a building: the Wednesday 20:55 watch is in the log in her hand, and the Tuesday 02:52 sound is a Tuesday-night entry in a building log, and the difference between those two sentences is the whole of his week. At 23:40, alone at the case while she fetches a tray, Elias puts his hands on the glass and gets four seconds of a gate in rain and a tired man calling out headcount figures a thousand times in a second, ending on a total he cannot catch. He comes round on the floor bleeding from one nostril and lies badly to her about a door push and a head condition. **The headache starts at 23:41 and is clocked from the start, because a cost you cannot time is a cost nobody has to believe in. It breaks at 10:42 Thursday, eleven hours and one minute later, in a shop on Lantern Row (Chapter 5).**

## Chapter 0004 — The Card With Two Names

Thu 01:00 – 04:00, inside the single Wednesday-evening-through-Thursday-morning shift. The plinth 34 card is hand-cut, not printed, so it was made in the label room. At 01:26 he puts his card on the locked Hall door out of pure habit and it logs the refusal — the first thing that night that puts him in the building's record, and he did it to himself, for nothing. He signs the device book at 01:52 (his own name, no destination), takes his phone from slot twelve in front of the duty manager on her round at about two, and signs it back at 02:41. In the label room at 02:05 — the lintel camera covers the doorway and the card cutter, not the drawers — he takes out the **spare** card for plinth 34 and finds it has been used before: two lines on the back, the maker's line taken off thoroughly by somebody who knew what a foundry catalogue is, and a second line gone at badly and not gone at all. **Wendel Case**, a person's name, in the line where a card keeps the person who put the thing in the case. He photographs it four times and puts it back half a millimetre out of true, deliberately, as a signal to whoever comes next. **Then, at 02:20, he card-reads the restricted door on the level-two archive corridor** — no camera on the landing, but a door that writes his name down — and finds that the Museum personnel record is paper, not a system: annual ring binders on a steel shelf behind the fire plan, one per year from 1974, every year present, every binder square, nothing disturbed. No W. Case, and no leaver's record for Case either. The horror is that the record is *undamaged* — grade 2 porter has a leaver's column with eleven tidy names in it and there is no seam anywhere in thirty years. He puts the binders back square, which is the only thing in that corridor that will be where he found it, and ends the night understanding that he has crossed into the behaviour he would have arrested someone for, and that telling Mara would make the card disappear on an index nobody can produce.

## Chapter 0005 — One Frame Short

Thu 19:00 – Fri 00:15, Ivo's systems night. Elias works out that Case has no line in current or archived Museum payroll, but has appeared for eighteen months on the municipal payroll bureau's monthly remittance statement, grade 2, with pension and union deductions still taken — proof of existence and of money moving, not proof the Museum's books ever carried him. Case's locker (81, in a run of forty-one numbered 61–101) is still in the basement: a hook, a half-full tin of tea, a comb, a folded newspaper, unlatched, and the erasure propagated to a printed locker card that Elias had read over for two years. The corridor stills show a shape at 01:12 on ten of fourteen nights, five arrival timestamps, two on the seventh night back and three on the fourteenth night back, all inside four seconds of one another, with a fifth-of-a-second smear on the two nights the figure was least complete. Elias buys eleven minutes of unauthorised corridor time with Ivo's complicity — 22:14 to 22:25 — and the door never opens. The leaf relocks a second and a half after the fault, and the eleven minutes are spent at a shut door in an ordinary corridor with a bracket that has no bell on it, and he gets nothing at all. Worse, the panel's event history for door 7 holds nothing for that night: no start, no restore, no log, because the controller gave up on the handshake, and the whole three-year history of that door is nine *restored* lines with no *started* in front of them. **Ivo's price is not a favour: he makes Elias say the arrangement out loud in the security room before he agrees, so that it can be repeated back to somebody later.** Ivo's silence then costs nothing, because there is nothing to suppress, and Elias understands that the wing was designed so that a man can stand at a locked door for as long as he likes and the building will never once say a word about it. The corridor ends at 22:25; **the watch on plinth thirty-four starts at 23:40**. Waiting for a pattern produces nothing; at 00:04 he writes *NO SOUND, THU*; at 00:05 the bell answers with the case in frame; the notebook entry is at 00:12 and the chapter closes at 00:15. **The chapter does not decide it for him.** It could have been late; it could be that a thing which only happens when you are not looking is a thing that happens when you are not looking, and no column headed *time / location / observation / action* can hold that without inventing a reason a thing happened when the reason is that you wanted it to. It could equally be coincidence, which he writes down too and does not believe, because a notebook holding only the conclusions you like in it is a list of wishes. Both possibilities sit side by side for the rest of the night, and both have the same consequence for Monday morning: there is no schedule to log.

## Chapter 0006 — Sillick Lane

Fri 09:00–14:00, alone, and he leaves the queue at 12:20 with a shift behind him rather than stay and be thorough. He goes down to the Museum's grade-2 posting sheets, which he found in the level-two card index on Thursday night, in Chapter 5, and which route every night porter of that decade to Sillick Lane, walks the length of the row to the mill site at the bottom of the street — eleven hundred metres of corrugated hoarding, a gate at each end, and **HALLOWGATE HOLDINGS** sprayed on the fence at head height — and then goes back up to the Friday unpaid-bills queue in the back room of the Sillick Lane branch library. The mill papers and the mill land are in the same pair of hands. Hattie Bramm, 54, Sillick Mill's gate clerk on the closing shift, holds a hard hand, a folder, and nine years of letters. She gives him the scale of the grievance — sixty-one people short-paid on the final pay run, a pay figure and not a headcount — and tells him the mill's own shortfall sheet was sold with the office papers to a museum on the hill that does not need them. Elias never gives her his job title; she supplies the Museum herself from her letter file. **Cliff Sowerby**, a fitter's mate at the dye house, lived at number 14 and mended the standpipe every autumn for a pint he would not take — he is a Low Ward neighbour and deliberately *not* a second Wendel Case; the name must not drift back. Elias's private wound is outranked, in a plastic chair, by somebody else's unpaid money.

## Chapter 0007 — The Roster

Fri 19:00–21:10. Elias finds 1961/LW/44 in the catalogue — *Sillick Mill: administrative papers, disposal, level B bay 9* — tries the sub-level alone, is refused by the gate interlock, and does not do anything stupid: he calls Ivo down as a second person, stands in front of the box with a torch, and leaves it unopened. The municipal payroll terminal logs his search and returns *no current record, no archived record*, and no leaver's record either. Anja Ward reads his Wednesday access log back to him in a corridor — 01:26 at the locked Hall door on a card that opened nothing, 02:05 in the label room on the first floor, under the lintel camera, 02:20 on level two at a restricted door that logs, 02:41 at the service entrance, against a Hall master key back on its hook since 23:52 — and gives him authorisation rather than information (work order 4412-S, 23:40–00:30, signed by a director, logged under Ivo's login). She tells him she would rather have a bad paragraph on her desk Monday than a find in an insurance file, and mentions the one restricted document in the building that predates her — an eighteen-year-old Quiet Wing card-access instruction signed by a name that appears in no list of people who have ever worked here. Elias decides not to ask. He also decides not to ask Ivo for anything, because asking would make it a favour.

## Chapter 0008 — Quiet Wing, After Hours

Fri 23:40 – Sat 01:05. With a director's signature holding the fail-secure leaf released and the corridor camera on the same circuit, Elias takes his phone out of the unlocked tray without signing (the book is right; it says the phone went home) and waits, doing nothing, until 23:52. He walks ninety feet into the corridor and comes to on the floor with his hand flat on a shut, latched door. Through clouded wired glass: a swivel chair with no dust on the seat, a coat hook, a shelf of Museum lanyards with every name panel blank, dust on everything else, and cold air under the door. **He gets the Index's second line as an interior line, with no source and no speaker, exactly as in Chapter 2 — and this time without the tinnitus excuse available — and does not write it in either book.** He photographs the corridor at 00:12 and writes one notebook entry at 00:18: **one entry, two halves, in two visibly different hands, which he cannot afterwards put in the order he wrote them and has never been able to.** He refits the old reader at 00:27 and is through the door before the controller answers at 00:28 — a logged forty-seven-minute outage inside a fifty-minute window. At 00:25 the hall bell and the sluice ward-bell's wrong strike arrive together through the floor, with a pressed-brass corridor service bell on the ward-bell repeater line repeating half a tone out of tune. At 00:30 Ivo comes back up with the new module still in his hand, finds the frame already live with the old unit, and writes nothing down; Elias is on a stair landing and does not call out. The chapter's cost is **sixteen minutes that are on the page in nobody's hand at all**: the last four minutes before the photographs are the ones he can account for, and the sixteen before them are in neither half of the entry and in neither order. He comes down off the landing at 00:50 and, because he has worked out that a thing nobody writes down is a thing that never happened, he opens the notebook and puts the Friday 00:25 bell down for the first time — with the sluice bell, the hall bell and the brass staff-call bell all in one line, and the note that the repeater is not the source of anything and is the plumbing that carries it.

## Chapter 0009 — The Log He Filed

Sat 07:00–12:30, then to 20:50. Dael, in since 06:30, builds a fault report out of a maintenance record that is entirely true and asks for one line: that access to the corridor during the outage was not established. Dael hands him the whole unvouched window instead of a suspicious one — 23:52 to 00:28, thirty-six minutes — and Elias's recognition is not that Dael picked it on purpose but that **a duration is not a witness**: the print accounts for the night continuously, the systems saw nothing, and sixteen of those minutes are in nobody's hand and never will be. The device book is a drawer away and neither man mentions it. Elias signs, having read exactly which four words are false. Dael cancels Saturday's forty-one-child tour on a maintenance notice and pays for it in donor calls and registrar letters, and gives Elias a cover story for plinth 34 that is not a lie. At 09:31 Elias rings his mother from the landing and tells her three untruths in three sentences about his hours and the letter. In Ivo's workshop — **Ivo's own Saturday systems day, and not rostered for the floor** — he builds a sheet-steel cover, four clamps, and the first salt-and-ash anchor card; they write the five-line protocol, and the stop word is *fault*, which is Ivo's word and which Elias gives to Mara as well in Chapter 10 so that a reading does not depend on one person having the right thought. Ivo takes the private duplicate notebook out of his hand, hands it back, and tells him exactly what he is doing. Mara reads the five lines first, agrees to be the person who can stop a reading on two conditions — the result in the shared log in her hand exactly as it comes, and he tells her everything every time — and refuses in advance to be alone in the building with a faulted west range. She says ten minutes; **Elias writes TEN MINUTES on the card, rubs it out, and writes 11 MIN instead, on the argument that a number she has to argue with is safer than one he agreed to in advance and then broke quietly. She writes eleven; he promises to stop at ten.** He agrees to both conditions, in writing, and knows what he has promised. He goes back on at 19:30.

## Chapter 0010 — Ninety-Four

Sat 20:50 – Sun 00:50. On the one night the building is full — cleaners, conservation tech, facilities, duty manager Priest on the freight lift, Ivo in on a favour — Elias runs the protocol on the busiest night of the week because it is the only night with a door anybody can open. Sixth note at 21:07. At 21:14 the third ward-bell at South Sluice fails outright: the west range drops to the emergency circuit, the Quiet Wing readers re-key, and the hall bell repeats on a fixed interval four times in six minutes. Mara comes down at 21:25 and writes eleven minutes on the card where she said ten, which Elias notices and does not hide. The reading runs 21:30–21:41: one question, a written time limit, a named cost, two witnesses, a salt-and-ash card, and *fault* — a word both Ivo and Mara now hold — said by Mara at 21:41 when the hall door, its strike plate riding again since Wednesday, swings four inches on the draught from the propped service door. It returns a gate in rain, a tired man reading a headcount aloud, a rope texture hauling across his palm, and **ninety-four**, which he says out loud in a hall that is not empty. Perry Loach, a night cleaner in his early sixties and a Sillick Mill fitter, answers the number without being asked in a voice he has not used in thirty-one years, and stops as though interrupted. Elias is not the only person the object can reach, and there is no line on his card for that. **The debrief now includes the form's occupancy line, which Mara completes truthfully as *one cleaner*, and the box beneath it headed *account of any person present during result*, which she correctly leaves blank** — it takes a voluntary statement in the man's own words, and she will not take one off a night cleaner at a plinth without his supervisor, and she will not put a guard's second-hand version in a box that wants the man's words, because the day she records what a person said to a guard in a guard's handwriting she has stopped keeping a record and started keeping a rumour. Elias has the name in ninety seconds at a service desk and does not supply it, because a name on that sheet starts a record. **The volume's central escalation now reaches a document, and the document cannot represent it, and the box will stay blank unless somebody goes and asks the man.** **Thirty-one hours after he wrote a page in two hands on the corridor floor, and on his way down to the service desk to ask Priest who the cleaner is, he catches himself holding the two facts together as one fact and understands that they are not one fact, and writes nothing down about it.** Then the batch's worst thing happens, and it happens inside the protocol rather than against it: Mara rules that her log **does not take a bare figure** — if she cannot say what a figure is a figure *of*, the result field gets *a number*, because a figure with an honest answer under it is a record and a figure with a guess under it is a rumour she will not sign. She writes **Result: a number** and initials it. Elias could have said *mill* and given her an object; he has a box on level B and a catalogue number and nine seconds on a terminal, and he does not know that it is this mill. **Four seconds pass and he lets the procedure do it for him, because a fact in a file with her name on it is a fact he cannot be held to, and a fact in a coat pocket is a fact he can be held to by nobody at all.** He puts his hand in to the notebook and hands her an empty one instead. Both of her Chapter 9 conditions are still technically unbroken, and that is the difficulty. At 00:20, lawfully on the floor in the loading bay while a crew of two stages eleven damp boxes off level B, he reads a hand-written label on a dust-sheeted box: *Sillick Mill: administrative papers / 1961 LW 44 / **PF 118*** — a live personnel file reference, not the porter's, on a box somebody moved out of bay nine in the last twenty-four hours. He ends on a situation, with no foreknowledge of his own suspension: a guard who cannot get to Monday's re-inspection, and somebody else in the building searching for the same man.

## Chapter 0011 — What It Was Kept From

Sun 07:00 – 21:00. Dael does the suspension in person at the service lane, properly, and the restriction is a printed sheet with the truth split in two: **withheld**, night duty and all rota allocation; **retained**, building keys, current card, the service lane and loading-bay desk, the security room, ground floor, staff facilities, and *attendance at any re-inspection of an object to which the holder has prior recorded access as escort, ground floor only, and not in the room*. Barred: the Hall of Former Faiths, the Gallery of Unnamed Hands, the conservation floor, the Quiet Wing. Review on the fourth Monday, no appeal before it, and in the payroll system the restriction is recorded as *scheduled leave in lieu of notice* — unpaid, and un-dismissible. Dael says out loud, once, that he did not get Elias, he got the systems, the systems are on his side and always will be, and that he would write the same four lines about a loading-bay shutter. The week is a man standing very close to a building he cannot walk into. He spends the day outside it, learns from the noticeboard that he is in the **second column** of Monday's re-inspection sheet, goes to the laundrette and does not open the letter on the shelf above the boiler — and says the sentence out loud once so that it is a thing that has been said, and promises to ask properly **after** he has told Mara he lied to her, and *in that order*, because "you'll be in the right order." He reads the Museum's plate on the portico for the first time in two years. No sound all day; the silence is the change in the world. **Ivo arrives at 14:40 with a toolbag, having run a systems inventory nobody asked him to run**, and says the insurance schedule is the document that matters, and Elias does not ask him for anything.

## Chapter 0012 — The Gallery That Is Not Keeping Its Own Count

Mon 07:00 – 23:00, sixteen hours on an unpaid day. Among two hundred and ten dead letters, the Museum's own letter of **9 October 1976** to *Mrs D. Sarn*, returned, forwarded twice, pencilled *Deceased — family*, with a fourth-floor forwarding address — which is how Elias gets a door to knock on. He copies it out by hand because the printed line over the tray says no photography of collections material, labels or records, and he does not want a new way of breaking the same rule in the same week. **The 09:00 re-inspection happens without him and he gets it secondhand.** Mara reads out the Registry's *QA/INSP/4* serial **QA/4/1187**: *Schedule 12 — carried object, no activity observed in the period of inspection*, and the note that a sound reported by Museum staff before the date of the inspection **was not observed**, and the assessor makes no finding as to the prior report. The case is **re-anchored**: new isolation pads, a lead to the building earth, and two brass floor plates stamped **QA/A/2** — the only brass on the floor of thirty-one plinths, whose entire function is to make the object *located*. **The card is photographed** through the glass with a scale rule, four exposures, on a Registry body camera, described as *hand-cut, ink, recent, second line erased, partial, number is not in the form of a Museum reference, not for reproduction* — so the erasure is now documented twice and the name is in nobody's file. The object is re-entered on the Museum's schedule as **unaccessioned, carried, on deposit**, with a depositor's agreement in which clause nine forbids removal without the depositor's consent and the depositor's address is the Registrar's office, so the Museum can no longer prove it owns it — **and the schedule now carries a `duly exported` flag set on 12 October on an object four floors up**, which is the single most effective thing done to that building in a hundred and forty years. Ivo rings: the **schedule of collections has been empty since 11 September** because the Registry has it. The bell is caged in a corridor off the west lavatories on a docket reading `PLACED BY TRANSFER: 20:00, Sat 16 Oct` — **signed, dated in the past, and left unmoved**, because a person who moved it would have had to destroy the form. The Chapter 10 headache breaks at 21:50. **The second reading:** **22:10–22:25** — in at 22:00, four minutes in the room at 22:04 — in the Gallery of Unnamed Hands, a room he is barred from, on a fitter's lamp, **1961/LW/51**, whose brass record strip carries a ballpoint margin entry — *Purchased at the closure of the works, 30 Oct 1974 — D. SARN*. The bound question is *who carried this lamp out of the last building it was in*, and the answer is a kitchen: a scrubbed table, a hard hand, a sheet of ruled paper, a figure entered twice, a woman saying *you'll have to ask the clerk, love*, and *put it in the post, second post, it's got to be the second post* — and **a man at a metre and from the side with no face on him at all**, because a lamp on a table cannot see a face. The last act is a sheet folded in three under a cloth, and a sentence the lamp kept for thirty-one years: **"That'll do, then."** Then the confirmation, from the room's own open drawer 6B: a **1974 carbon receipt** listing eleven items collected on the last working day by *P. LOACH (fitter)* off Sillick Lane, item 9 the lamp, item 10 *bundle of wage sheets, tied with tape, marked pay office*, received from **Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane** — in fifty years, never opened. Elias does not photograph it and knows exactly why: a thing in a Museum file on a night, in a suspended man's hand, is not evidence. **The count in the room: nine objects, forty-one drawers.** *The room is not keeping its own count* — the batch's Index line, unaccounted for, written nowhere. Stage 1 is confirmed here and it is not a triumph.

## Chapter 0013 — The Fifth Card

Tue 08:20 – 21:30. The eight photographs have nowhere to go, and the batch's first real work is a negotiation: he rules out Mara (handing over would make the broken promise *look* paid), Ivo (not allowed, and Ivo has a presentation and a name of his own on a fault report), the police (there is no crime he can name in a sentence), and then a fourth option he writes down and does not cross out: *nobody*. He asks, finally, and Ivo's inventory minute is the answer: the **schedule of collections is empty since 11 September** because the Registry has it, so *the institution in this city with a complete itemised list of what this building holds is not the Museum* — and the fire compartmentation note has been improved by the assessor, so containment is better and the claim is worse. Fenn. In the label room, at 16:10, with a junior guard outside the door who signed the book. The Sillick drawer has been closed and squared since two o'clock in the afternoon: **somebody read his crooked-card signal and answered it.** She tells him the card is the **fifth** sleeve from the front, not the third, because eleven sleeves and two of them are Wednesday's blanks and four behind are the Gallery's re-card — and she does it to stop him building a tidy false story. She refuses twice, correctly: she will not name the person who cut the card, *because a name on a card is a thing that follows a person*, and she will not say she let him in, because she did not, and she quotes her own exact sentence back to him: *the Low Ward series are two drawers down on the left, and don't take mine.* **The first time anybody says no to him and means it.** He writes a 400-word statement in which he breaks himself first — the two device-book entries, the two rooms, the displaced card, the signed false cause — and seals four photographs in a Museum envelope addressed in block capitals **to the Board Secretary, not to the conservation floor, not to the Registrar**, by registered post with a receipt, and has the day book entered and initialled at 18:24 by Priest. Dael finds him at 20:45, tells him the claims manager is **Cheryl Vosper at Vail & Thwaite, who is not a lawyer and has a drawer**, and that *leave in lieu of notice* means he cannot be sacked and cannot be paid. **Then he posts a second envelope.** The claims address is printed at the foot of the fault report form he falsified, in a box in the post room marked *CLAIMS — POST ROOM COPY*, and the address of the one person in the world who cannot be reached through this building has been in eleven-point type in front of him for two years. Four photographs, a second typed copy of the four hundred words, registered at the counter on Sillick Lane at about half past nine with a receipt required, and Mara Iles comes down to the service entrance with the envelope and writes nothing down. He wanted two copies and got a fact instead. Index: *A copy has been given to a person who was not named in it.* Close on the recognition that nobody in the building is protecting the thing — everyone is protecting their own floor — and that Ivo did it anyway and was never asked.

## Chapter 0014 — The Woman Who Kept a Copy

Wed 09:00 – 22:00. `12 Ferry Bank`, second floor, first door. Doreen Sarn, 78, Sillick Mill pay clerk, a cardigan over a pinafore, a net curtain and a plastic tumbler on the sill because the china is in the press. Three documents on a cloth: the 1976 letter, his copy of the Gallery's nine-entry inventory, and the 1974 receipt line by line out loud. On the seventh entry her finger stops for slightly longer than on the others and comes on, and he does not know why for four days. The lamp on her table is **the mate** of the one in the Museum, and she bought hers off the forecourt on the last morning for **two shillings** and has had it in the press for **fifty years**. She indicts herself before he can: *"I was good at it. That was the whole of the trouble. I could make a column add up out of nothing, and when the foreman gave me a figure I asked him one question and he gave me another, and I wrote the one he asked me for twice, and the sheet balanced."* And the thing that opens the trail: **the shortfall sheet went out in the second post on 22 October 1974, in her own writing, with a proper stamp** — and the mill's post came back to the mill when the mill closed, and the office cleared it into a sack, and **the sack went in the van** — so the 1974 receipt's *envelope of loose papers, sealed, contents not examined* is very probably her own envelope, and has never been opened in fifty years. He does not tell her what the lamp saw, and she notices that he has *the lamp saw* in his mouth and lets him off. He gives her the record-address fact: the Museum's letting agent was the postal address for grade-two porters from 1998 for a decade, and a letting agent is a post office in a building. **Quill & Frayle ring the door at 16:10 and are let in at 16:30, because Doreen telephoned them herself to be introduced rather than found out.** Mr Rhys is reasonable, documented and immovable: the site clears on **4 November**; the 1976 clearance schedule exists and he cannot hand it over; the file number on her 1976 letter is the same one in her folder, and their office has been at 14 Lincoln's Lane for fifty years; and a tribunal, not a front room, is what a document schedule is for. Close: her fifteen-word reconciliation with her own guilt, and then the Monday bag on the loading-bay desk — his registered envelope going out of the door, and eleven years of a dead man's post going out of the same door in the same bag, to a paper merchant, to a pulp mill, because the line at the head of the book says *post is received, not opened, not kept* and he has been signing next to it for two years.

## Chapter 0015 — A Lift and Not a Removal

Thu 09:00 – 20:00. **Anja's Director's standing conditions**, four of them, issued at 10:00: docket and countersignature; **no removal except on a docket countersigned by a person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum**; no public access between a proposed disposition and its execution, with a ten-working-day lapse; and **the Hall of Former Faiths shall not be opened to a school group while a carried object in it is caged, moved, or out of its last certified condition.** Anja says plainly that she has spent nothing on him in four days and that this is not generosity — he is the cheapest thing in the building to leave alone — and that she has been instructed by two solicitors to do the thing she cannot tell him about. **The Registry notice QA/N/34** in four lines: works at sub-level B completed; third ward-bell out of service, **will not return this quarter**; *district lock positions: unchanged*; Museum action: none. The consequences arrive inside a minute: the ward-bell repeater line is dead, so the pressed-brass service bell in the unlisted corridor goes quiet for the first time in eighteen years, silently, with no fault and nobody coming. **The device tray is taken out of service** and replaced by a one-line-per-device day book at the front desk — a genuine improvement, on the strength of a review about photographs, which means the Museum has *reduced* its own ability to prove that a telephone was in anybody's hand, and the four photographs are weaker than they were on Tuesday. Then, in the tray, under a circular and a bank statement, an envelope addressed **MR W. CASE, 14 MILL LANE, LOW WARD**, forwarding sticker expired six weeks ago, and because post for this building is opened by two members of staff it is a Museum record: *you have had his money since the new year… there is a bit of paper in the tin with figures on it that you said you would send him and never did… **we know about the money.*** Signed **I. Case**, writing for a father who cannot write, at number 4 Sillick Lane since the year the mill shut, who did the last count at the gate on the Friday morning, and who *will not have it on his record*; and the money still going to a dead wife's name since 1994. Elias stands with his hand on **locker 81** — brass plate, unlatched, never locked, nothing about it ever having required a decision — and does not open it, because the moment he opens it the tin belongs to him, and *a second time he decides alone, in a room, that somebody is safer not knowing*. He goes upstairs and tells Mara everything instead, and she takes the letter to the Board Secretary and the audit provider, and asks the Board in writing for authority to open a leaseholder's locker, and states in her covering note that **no person in this building may decide on their own that somebody is safer not knowing.** The levelling sheet: **AWAITING TRANSFER — H. & L. Foyston, Northglass**; site clearance 4 November; contents deemed chattels and fixtures of the works; bailiffs appointed; the three returned *not delivered* letters in the tray; the red custody disc with **W. Ward**; and the day book's printed head, *THE BOOK IS THE RECORD. THE TRAY IS NOT*, above his own 00:24 entry from the seventh of October. **The bin goes out at 07:15 on Friday.** He cannot stop it, and the reason he could not is not fear: a man who rings a post room at seven to hold one bale out of a hundred and fifty is a man who has admitted he has been reading the post.

## Chapter 0016 — What Is in the Cupboard

Fri 07:15 – 20:00. **The second breach is made here.** First, the bin: it went at 07:15 and a man with a hand truck wrote a figure in a box and left. Then the library. Hattie has a file with him in it — a stranger came in three weeks ago with a photograph of a cat and said a name out loud twice — and Siobhan Keogh's name is not spoken in that room. In the queue and out on the pavement she shows him, for the first time to anybody, a letter from the site's new owner offering **a review of the final shortfall at the company's cost, without admission of liability, on the basis that her correspondence ceases for twelve months** — a considered number, a week of nothing, and the only reason it is worth anything is that it has never been priced. She has not answered it and told nobody. He says: **nobody in the world knows, and it stays that way, and I am not going to tell anybody, and if anybody asks me whether anyone has had an offer, the answer is that I have never heard of it.** She makes him say it again and look at her while he says it. Then she asks where it is, and he tells her. **The Hall of Former Faiths, ground floor, the case at the end of the row on the east side, under a brass surround, a bronze bell the size of a fist with a staple through a crack in the shoulder, cast 1902, in a cage by the lavatories with the Director's initials on it** — eleven seconds, in a street, in front of a glass door, with eleven people behind it. He had four seconds in which to say *I can't tell you that*, and he did not, and the reason he did not is a table in Ferry Bank with a hardback cash book on it and a woman who has had the only copy in the world for fifty years and nobody to give it to: **the private channel keeps closing because he keeps closing it.** He refuses to say a number, and refuses to say a name, and Hattie says the name for him — *there's a Case at number four* — and will not say it either, and tells him it is not caution, it is the price she pays for being the person in the room who knows. Then, at one, the room empties, and the man by the door is still there: a man in his fifties in a plain dark coat with a shopping bag and a tin of tea in it and a hearing aid he does not turn on and a piece of paper with nothing on it. **Elias does not go and ask him.** He stands three feet away for ten seconds and walks out, because the man has not asked him anything, and because a man who says a number at a plinth at ten on a Saturday night has not consented to be asked, and because asking needs a form and a supervisor and the man's own hand, and because he watched a woman with a stopwatch decline to fill in exactly that box for exactly those reasons. Hattie watches him walk past and says nothing, **which is the most damning thing anybody does to him all week.** Close on the arithmetic: four locks, every one of them turned by him.

## Chapter 0017 — Attendant: Two

Sat 12:00 – Mon 01:00. A registered letter, served at 11:20, **NOT BEFORE 10:00 / HOLD FOR SIGNATURE**: the clearing programme has been accelerated and the site will be complete by Sunday night. He works it out in nine seconds at ten past one — **the cage cost a clearing contractor a fortnight, and a card with a ten-working-day lapse on it is what cost the site the money.** Ivo rings from the conservation floor: Foyston's transfer clerk has been in the Hall, read the number off the cage tag, and written it in a book; there is a **folding ladder in the west range corridor**; a floor plan on a trolley; the corridor measured; the cage fouling the swing of a lavatory door, *noted*, and nothing done about it, because it is not his job. Elias is off rota and trespasses at half past one. **The gate is open thirty inches, deliberately.** The slipway is under six inches of water since the spring because the pumps were switched off when the site was fenced, and the pumps came out that morning. On the tailgate of the lorry, on a consignment note, in a box: **ATTENDANT: 2 (TWO)** — and there is one man on that slipway. **Cliff Sowerby, 74, of number 14 Sillick Lane**, whose father was the dye-house fitter's mate, who kept a key to a steel box on a shelf behind the site-office meter, and who went up the ramp because the office was at the top and the ramp was the way to it and a man in a high-visibility jacket with his back to him did not turn round. The scaffold board went. Four minutes in six-degree water. Elias throws the rucksack behind him, gets a coil of line off a nail in a first aid box in the office doorway, round a handrail post, and says *fault* out loud to nobody in an empty yard, and a woman from the site cabin sees the whole of it. The line goes round the old man's good arm. Cliff Sowerby talks the entire time he is in it. He is got up by the brigade and a paramedic says two words that change the shape of the day. **The number that is wrong is not a headcount and not a wage: it is two men required to attend a lift, filled in by a young man in a good coat in an office on a Friday, and nobody has ever read a consignment note back after the event.** Then the police, at half past four: his name on a form for an accident, a crime number, and he gives them the true version — that he was trespassing, that nobody sent the old man, that the ramp has no rail and the rail is off and the staging has a board on it, and that the note said two men and there was one. Site closure: **load 340, Low Ward, 16 November** — the laundry. He goes back in on a shift that is not his and tells Priest to ring Doreen Sarn, because she is the only person in the city who can say what was on that slip, and it was paper, and a man is in an ambulance. And then, in an empty loading bay, to nobody: **I didn't ask him. I never asked him.** Two hundred and six dead letters, because it is the only job he has never got wrong.

## Chapter 0018 — The Two-Hands Thing

Sun 10:00 – 22:00. The emotional floor. He tells his mother on a bench that he lied to Mara's face on Saturday night, and she says she is going to be very good about it, that he will come out feeling better and will not have been forgiven, and those are three separate things and he has only booked one — and that when Mara asks why, he will say it was to protect somebody, and that is true, and it is the worst answer in the world. **Hattie Bramm, at number 14 on Sillick Lane**, next door to a man in hospital: *you've killed it* — not the letter, the *offer*, because a hoist in a photograph is the last thing that district will ever hear the word *review* after, and it was only hers while it was unpriced. She tells him the reason he cannot stop Sowerby going up a slip is that he does not know what is at the bottom of it. She keeps the polaroid, in a house on Sillick Lane, with a man of seventy-four in a bed; **not in your building, not with me**; and *the day the two of us want the same thing we'll find each other without a lawyer between us.* **Doreen Sarn, Ferry Bank, half past one, with the fire lit since nine.** He tells her the whole of the reading, and at the end: *did it show my writing? — Then it's a document. Because a document is a thing a person wrote, and the hand's on it, and you can't get that out of a lamp.* And: *you'd better not be the only one who can do it. Because if you're the only one, then by Christmas there's a hundred people in this city who need you to do it… and there'll be nobody to say the word.* He said the word on Monday in an empty room and there was nobody there. The *Chronicle*'s Sunday picture is a lorry on a slipway; cut off at the bottom of the column, much smaller, a **weighbridge gate** in 1998 and a caption, and *Hallowgate Foyston said the site had no significant features of architectural interest* — a gate photographed, on the day the last of the paper came off the site. **The label room, half past four, escorted by the registrar herself, who signs the book at the service entrance in the escort column and does not make a joke about it, which alarms him more than anything.** She reads the Saturday protocol back to him exactly as filed — *Result: a number. Occupancy line: one cleaner. Account of any person present during result: [blank]* — and says it is accurate, is in the file for ever, and there is nothing wrong with it anywhere. She reads the car-park record of the sealed envelope and the fact that she has read the day book every day since the tenth. Her covering note of 18 October, 15:40, to the Board Secretary and the audit provider: the returned letter is not to be opened or circulated; a written instruction is requested for locker 81; *a name should be entered in the account box, withheld in this note, because the file is a document and a name in a document is a thing that follows a person* — and the name is Wendel Case, given in a covering note, on a Saturday, in a room with a table, to a member of the Board and nowhere else. **He says the sentence: he lied to her face, twice over, and he did it because a fact in one pocket is a fact nobody can take off you.** *I am not going to forgive you.* Two conditions: nothing in a coat, and **nobody in this building decides on their own that a person is safer not knowing** — and at the stair door: *we are not us. There is a protocol and there are two conditions and one of them is a relationship, and the relationship is that there is no more private channel.* And the two things she will not do: ring Hattie Bramm, and let him write a word of the form that has to be built for Perry Loach — *I can. Not you, and not in a week, and not at ten at night on a Monday.* And a standing appointment: **Monday, half past two, notebook against file** — and a figure in his notebook that is not in her file, and he has to decide what to say.

## Chapter 0019 — Standing Instruction 14

Mon 09:00 – 22:00. **Standing Instruction 14 (2024) — Objects in Transit: Suspended Quarantine**, four pages, three signatures, and two clauses that cancel each other out. Clause three: returned correspondence is held twelve months then destroyed, no member of staff may act on one without the Director's written authority, and *the existence of a returned item of correspondence is not of itself evidence that the addressee is or was a member of staff.* Clause four: a locker whose plate does not correspond to a current member of staff is recorded, its contents held pending enquiry for twelve months, and **a locker is not a Museum record and its contents are not Museum property.** Clause five: photographing collections material is disciplinary. Clause six: **no document, image, record or copy of Museum material may be lodged in any Museum file, Board file or departmental file the Museum has not itself requested, without the Director's written authority.** And then, in the margin, initialled and dated Sunday at ten to four, **clause seven: the Museum will not destroy any record of an individual employee held by the municipal payroll bureau without first obtaining the bureau's own account in writing.** Two lines above it, clause six, and the registered-post envelope he put in the Monday bag on Tuesday is now inside a Museum file, in a building, four floors up, because a registered-post item is delivered to a building and signed for at a desk. He works it out in four minutes standing at a desk. **Redgate & Kell** at half past ten, two men, one sheet: *we're not here about the fall.* He tells them where the cage is, because the cage is a fact and a transfer clerk wrote *noted* about it, and gets *thank you* for the second time this week from a person he has given nothing to. **Cheryl Vosper** at noon, ringing the security room because a suspended night guard is a fact-finding resource: the fault-report summary is fine, and if the Board never reads anything else in the file they will be all right — and then: *there's a second page in the file, a photograph of a card, and a covering note of four hundred words in your own handwriting, and a sentence at the bottom of it that says if this is evidence it is evidence against me first, and I have written it so that it is*; it came in registered post to a claims address printed at the foot of a fault report form, and not to the building — **and there are two envelopes, and he tells nobody in this chapter which one is which, because the other one is four floors above him inside clause six and worth nothing**; the hearing is on the twenty-eighth; **you are not in that room, and I am asking you not to be.** Ivo's inventory, presented at seven in a room Elias was not in: the **schedule of collections restored**, eleven discrepancies, **one object whose location was corrected — physically found in a cage in a service corridor** — and the best line in it, a schedule note with a number in it, in the third section, about a bin count: *loading bay, cage 2, current position blocks the swing of a door on a designated route of escape.* Nobody has to be brave about a fire thing; they just move a cage. Dael comes in on his day off, says the docket is not his any more and that the cage has to move because he cannot be the man who moves it, and asks Elias to tell Ivo, and Elias does — and Dael says *that's the first useful thing you've done all fortnight* — and then tells him, off the record, that the records clerk's post has gone to systems and the woman who knew every binder is gone in March. Nunn at nine with a cage and a **witness form — *present at removal: name, grade, time, signature***, no *in whose hand* — and Elias fills it in, four hundred words, correcting the object identity (*this is not the object described in this docket*) and refusing to describe the contents, and then makes Nunn correct the *location* from *cage 2, west range* to *cage 2, service corridor*, because a wrong location in a removal docket becomes a wrong location in a fire register in four months. And then the payroll notice, and the third page of the pay clerk's clipboard: **the cleaners are not on the bureau. They are on a services contract, written in establishment, shift and hours.** The contract returns are a printed grid with cells filled in by hand and the head of it says **CLEANING — 0**, and it has an hour against the west range corridor, and an hour against the Quiet Wing corridor, and there is no column in the whole printed grid in which a person can be written — *and nine was the number the suppression had been using for thirty-six years, and it is not a lie, and it is not an accident, and it is not going to be a mistake that somebody made.*

## Chapter 0020 — Thirty-Eight Children and Two Teachers

Tue 08:20 – 20:00. The batch turn. The docket comes down at 08:20 with **NOT ON HOLD**, a date line four days in the future and a Saturday, Dael's signature on it, and a witness line that asks for **name, grade, time, signature** and not for the man's own words — so he does not fill it in, and puts a pencil notice in the day book instead, initialled, recording that on Sunday afternoon a member of the public asked him at that desk what the object was and what he said. Anja: clauses one to four satisfied, the movement is not a disposition, the docket is not signed and will not be signed by her or by Nunn, because the only thing standing between a bell and a van is the absence of a non-employee countersignature **and it is standing there by accident**; and she has spent the morning telephoning Northglass. **The bell goes back on plinth thirty-four at 10:50** — four people, nine minutes, a new seal, fresh pads, the lead, the crutch — and the crack is in the shoulder under the staple and nobody in the room says a word about it, and a woman in Northglass is going to certify it in four weeks as *a mechanical repair, not an act of veneration*, and she will be right. And Elias, thirty feet away at a desk, cannot go in: not because he is barred, but because he cannot be in a room with a man in overalls again. **Aurelia Roth** at 12:30 — a Registry assessor, thirty-four, six years in — does it all again, and the classification is **Annexe D**, a **permanent** classification, *a hand bell, unpitched at rest, no inscription, no dedication, no figure or emblem, the staple a mechanical repair and not an act of veneration, a plain bell with no maker's mark is not a Quiet Saint*, with **confidence: medium**, and one clause at the bottom that stops the whole volume: **the object is to be displayed; under no circumstances is it to be removed from public view by a museum acting alone.** She tells him the institution with the best document in it got there without seeing a single one of his documents, and that the object is attached to one local history and possibly to a claim, and that she has a form on the notice board that anybody may put in and has read four of them in six years and three of them are requests to have a painting re-hung. **Dael Ordway comes back onto the floor at 16:00.** He has not been on nights since the eleventh of October and is not the supervisor that week. He tells Elias to ring the transfer clerk off the conservation floor, and then says: *go and look at the booking book, and then bring it to me, and I will tell you why I have not taken four days off.* **The booking book** is a green ledger on the security desk that has been there for two years and nobody has read: **13 NOV, 11:00, Hall of Former Faiths, 38 children, 2 teachers, per term booking, St Anselm's (Hall Lane), booked 1 Sep, ref. SK/BK/0912.** Dael's move: the *if a public event is scheduled, the disposition is deferred until the event has taken place* clause, which he wrote himself, and a school group booked in September. **Annexe D is the best document this building has produced in my lifetime and it will hold that bell in that case for ever, and it will do nothing whatever for sixty-one people, because a classification is not a wage.** *I am going to be the man who wakes thirty-eight children up on a Friday morning in November, and I have never done anything else in eleven years that frightened me as much, and I am doing it, and I am not doing it for you.* Mara at 16:10 writes the registrar's re-certification of the Hall — **one sentence, Annexe C: *No undue deterioration has occurred since the last certification* — *a lie of omission of a size I have signed about four hundred times*** — and then refuses two things out loud: she will not ring Hattie Bramm, because a member of the public who telephones becomes a person in a Museum file, and because *asked* is the word a claim has been built on for thirty-one years; and she will not let him ask Hattie either, because a favour is what she has been fighting for nine years. Hattie comes up the service lane at 17:35 anyway, on a courtesy booking, is shown round by a third-floor officer with a nice office, and Elias stands on the second-floor landing with a clipboard and does not say one word to her as she walks past, **and it costs him more than the canal**. She comes out with four typed words — *a hand bell, unpitched at rest, no devotional content* — and one sentence she wrote on the back: *there is nobody on that street over fifty-five except one man of seventy-four who is in the hospital, and every one of them said the last day was a pay day, and the museum calls it a closure.* **And the sentence the whole batch ends on, said to one person:** the disposition does not go this weekend, it goes on **the twenty-ninth**, ten working days off a proposal posted at ten o'clock on Thursday the fifteenth, and on **Friday 30 October** the cage is a piece of furniture in a corridor and there is nothing in anybody's hand. Close: the last post, a pencil line in the day book, and then the booking book copied into his notebook and read aloud to Mara at 19:06 — **thirty-eight children and two teachers, booked on the first of September by a school, in ink, in a ledger that sits on a security desk eleven feet from where they were talking** — and the last line, with **ninety-two cut out**: in three weeks he is going to stand in a corridor outside a room and listen to a woman in overalls say a number, and after that he has to find out whether the first number was ever the only one, and whether there is a second one at all, **and he has absolutely no way of finding out.**

---

## Volume 01 Index

| Chapters | Title | Pressure carried |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1–3 | Night Round / The Bell in the Hall / A Number That Does Not Add | Ordinary competence, then the first recurrence, then the first involuntary contact |
| 4–5 | The Card With Two Names / One Frame Short | **Batch 0001 midpoint** — the case crosses from noise to *this building has renamed a person* |
| 6–7 | Sillick Lane / The Roster | Character and relationship; political and institutional pressure |
| 8–9 | Quiet Wing, After Hours / The Log He Filed | Escalation and dread; the falsification and the hard-won agreement |
| 10 | Ninety-Four | **Batch 0001 climax** — the bounded reading, the withholding, the relabelled box |
| 11 | What It Was Kept From | **The suspension** — character and consequence; no supernatural event |
| 12 | The Gallery That Is Not Keeping Its Own Count | **Batch 0002 spine beat** — the re-inspection, and the second reading that confirms the power and indicts a living person |
| 13 | The Fifth Card | Relationship — Ivo's inventory, Fenn's refusal, and the photographs going somewhere |
| 14 | The Woman Who Kept a Copy | Investigation and character, with an ordinary living person as the source |
| 15 | A Lift and Not a Removal | Political and institutional pressure |
| 16 | What Is in the Cupboard | **Character, relationship, and the moral turn** — the second withholding, made knowingly |
| 17 | Attendant: Two | **Action and survival** — the escalation, a real injury, a real loss |
| 18 | The Two-Hands Thing | Recovery and emotional payoff — the breach named out loud and not forgiven |
| 19 | Standing Instruction 14 | Political and institutional pressure — the same thing wearing better clothes |
| 20 | Thirty-Eight Children and Two Teachers | **Batch 0002 turn** — a decision and a date, the tour found rather than booked, and the disposition's own ten days running out |

**Costs on the page, Batch 0002:** Ch 11 a suspension with no authority in four rooms and a letter he does not open; Ch 12 a hot iron taste, hot palms, the rope gone from the right hand, a four-second gap at the answer, a fifteen-minute bound where the promise was eleven, a barred door and no witness and no card worth the name; Ch 13 four hundred words of self-indictment, four photographs given up, a rule broken that can no longer be proved, and a room's worth of work; Ch 14 two shillings and a sentence he cannot un-say; Ch 15 eleven years of a dead man's post in a blue bale, a tin he does not open, and a police number of his own; Ch 16 a room, a row, a plinth, a date and a case said aloud in a street in front of a glass door with eleven people behind it, **and a number he did not say and a name he did not spend**; Ch 17 four minutes in six-degree water, four stitches' worth of cold, a crime number, and a form with a consignment note on it; Ch 18 the whole of the reading told out loud, a name in a covering note, and the loss of the only private channel he had left; Ch 19 a clause that puts his evidence back inside the building and a grid with no column in it for a person; Ch 20 a witness line left blank, thirty-eight children booked for a Friday in November, and ten working days running out on a Thursday. **There is no second notebook entry and no second memory cost.** The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is not tidied, not stabilised and not repeated.

---

# Chapter Summaries — Volume 01, Batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30), "The Tour"

## Chapter 0021 — The Form

Wed 09:00 – 21:00. The occupation form is finished: one sheet, printed one side and typed on the other, **no boxes that want a guard's version of anybody.** Five provisions, of which the fifth is the only line about consent anybody in the city has ever printed: *Do you want anybody in the room with you while you fill this in? Yes / No / I do not know yet. You may change this answer at any time, including after you have handed it in.* **Elias reads it twice, does not sign it, and cannot say a good reason for not signing, only the true one.** Mara takes it to **Quarry Bank Solicitors, 4 Irving Quay** — not a panel firm, not the Board's, four rooms above a shop, and a partner who was a union shop steward for eleven years — and her four reasons include *if I die in a stairwell tomorrow then it is in a firm in Irving Quay and not in a Museum file.* **Redgate & Kell's third question is *is the object in the Hall of Former Faiths at this moment*, which is true, and which would have been a lie if he had said it, because at 06:20 the previous morning a conservator had wheeled a case out of a public gallery on a written instruction and nobody in the press office knew.** **Statement 3 goes out at 12:30 and is four true lines.** A **weighbridge poster** goes up in Lantern Row with one caption line and no explanation. At three o'clock **Ivo's new board, handrail and rule** go up in the west range corridor: *a party shall never include, in its tasking, any person assessed at Level 4 or above; if a party is required to do a job and cannot, the party does the job, and if it cannot, the party goes out.* **Nine evacuation parties, nine failures.** The last entry in the *fixed positions* file — last whole-building review **sixteen September, five weeks ago** — reads *west range service corridor, ground floor: one observer, fixed position, minimum distance three metres, no contact, at all times when the corridor is in use*, and nobody writes a name in a fixed-positions file. **The Hall of Former Faiths is shut to school parties, term and single, by 15:00, by a correct rule, applied correctly, on a Wednesday afternoon, by a man putting a board on a wall.** Dael finds out that the one card on the table has been turned over. Close: the form's consent line, and *it is the only line in it*, and a telephone number and a room he is allowed into at the bottom of it.

## Chapter 0022 — The Container

Thu 09:00 – 20:00. **PO-4471** lands on the terminal at 09:04: one sealed container, one low-loader, half a day of crane hire with *attendance 08:00*, and a note *quantity reduced to 1. Attendee count required on site.* A crane is not for putting a box on castors on a ramp; **somebody has bought half a day of a crane.** Elias cannot go on the site and does not. **Hattie Bramm is on the far pavement, for the ninth time in thirty-one years, and nobody comes out to throw her out, because they have a crane.** Twenty hourly men and a foreman do the work to a programme and **not one of them opens anything**, because nothing in it can be opened and a man who opens a thing he has been asked to move is a man who has opened a thing. Hattie says **the fourth mark** on the slipway wall and the foreman calls the pallets back onto the good board, and that is the whole of the ninth time. She makes the foreman **read the destination bay number out loud twice** so that a man on the gate writes it down and she dates it: **Quay 3, Broomhead Reach, bay 4, in bond** — *which is the same store the Museum's own cage was destined for*, and nobody remarks on it, and nobody ever will, because one list is an accession and one is a company schedule and they are both the same van. The container is shut at 14:20, a tag is read out, twenty men sign for it, **attendant: 2 (two)** is filled in at a yard by somebody who has never heard of a slipway. Hattie: *nobody up there has any idea what it was, and a seal is not a name.* Close: the escalation, stated — **the people who can now be hurt are the twenty-two, and the people who can no longer be reached are the hundred and forty.**

## Chapter 0023 — The List

Fri 07:00 – 22:00. **Low Ward police station, 14:20.** The caution is the event, not the statement: it is the first time in eleven days that a person with a statutory duty has said *you may be prosecuted* and written it down. **VPC Ainsley** asks for the table and not for the room, says she has not read the file and is not going to, and says *I want to stop* is a real sentence. The questions are about a fence, a rope, four minutes in six-degree water and a man of seventy-four, and they are good. Then the middle of it: he says **the count was in a reading**, and refuses to say whose, and the word *reading* is in a typed record in four sentences forever. Sergeant Ashby writes *the writer makes no finding as to its nature* and turns the pad face down. **Ivo arrives at half past one with a toolbag and four exposures of drawer 6B, open, in place, from the condition survey he was obliged to do in March** — 246 exposures, scale rule, tripod, in a systems index with his name on the sheet — and hands them over **in his own working capacity, without being asked, and asks for it to be written down that way.** Ainsley gives him a witness list of ten groups in the wrong order, on a plastic-wallet sheet, and says **you may take a name off this list yourself; you do not have to give a reason and nobody in this building will check; do it in your own hand.** **He strikes off H. Bramm and H. Loach.** Ainsley tells him the list is an invitation, not evidence, and *a woman who has just told you what it does is not your friend* — and is right. He leaves Cheryl Vosper four words on a voicemail: *you were right about all of it, not to me, to the file.* Close: eleven days, four photographs with a reference, a paragraph six he cannot take back, and **a name of a man's in a plastic wallet in his coat** where the volume's single most damaging figure used to be.

## Chapter 0024 — A Lawful Removal of the Wrong Thing

Sat 06:00 – Sun 25 Oct 01:00. The batch spine. The docket is executed correctly at six in the morning and **the thing that goes out of the building is a fitter's lamp in a sealed vitrine from plinth 9, and the box of eleven stays in the cage** — because the docket's third block, *countersignature required, a person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum*, is blank and cannot be filled, and the only block on the form that is not the clerk's is **PRESENT AT REMOVAL: name, grade, time, signature**. The crate goes up the tailgate ramp on two wedges and **comes back down it**: Elias writes five sentences into the signature space, the last of which is *that no removal took place*, and Nunn says he told the office the same thing on Tuesday, strikes his line, writes *did not travel* and initials it. The vitrine goes up in its place and Nunn records it as *offloaded in error, sealed, no opening*, and the destination on that line is the docket's, because a clerk does not improve on a document. **A lawful removal of the wrong thing is not sabotage, and a docket that cannot be countersigned is not a removal of anything.** An office in Northglass puts the lamp on a *return* at 07:00 with the docket's destination typed in, because that is the destination on the sheet they are working from; Elias corrects it **in writing at 10:20, initialled, timed, through the registered internal post so that it had a movement number**, and it is *a request and not an instruction and I have no authority to issue an instruction.* Four lines from a bonded store — line 1 did not travel, the container off the Sillick site is on the quay at BAY 4 — the last of them **PUT AWAY**, which does not mean a room: it means a cage. On the site a pump housing comes off the slip without a sling swinging, because a foreman moved a fourteen-year-old sixteen inches up a ramp on the strength of a telephone call from a call box — and then the boy backs up four feet to clear a hook and puts his wrist on a sawn end of timber, and gets a **fracture of the radial styloid**, and **nobody is to blame**, and his grandfather was a fitter at the Sillick, and he had asked for that site by name. Hattie Bramm is four feet away and says so out loud: *I told him twice and he did not hear me the first time because of the crane and the second because he was on the radio, and there was a woman with a clipboard four foot from him who did not move, and I could not make him move.* A registrar's five-clause protective-movement instruction moves the bell into the loading-bay office overnight, and signs it, and states the risk in writing: *in the Registry's judgement it may be a removal from public view, and if it is, then this Museum has done it.* Ivo builds the room and a cover and a card and **writes down the line for the case where nobody can say the word.** At **00:52 on Sunday 25 October**, with eleven people in a loading bay — nine of them there for a reason and two of them because there is no clause that says *and the room is empty* — Elias asks one question, in front of a flooring contractor's foreman who came for a signature and a night cleaner with a mop. The answer is a pay office, a third person at a counter with a hand flat on it, a gate-book box, a stopped clock reading **five minutes to six**, a folded blanket, and **twenty-four in the corner of a leaf that nobody may say what it counts.** The cost is paid: **four seconds in which he could not produce a name**, and for eleven minutes afterwards he cannot produce Hattie Bramm's surname. Close: *a man who chooses a price before he knows what it costs has not chosen a price*, and the notebook entry is the only page in the book crossed out in four places.

## Chapter 0025 — The Number Is a Wage

Mon 09:00 – 23:00. **The midpoint reversal.** At 09:00 the material-change return inspection of the bell under QA/CR/1187 is held, with a member of the public present — and the member of the public is Elias Venn, who gets in by signing a visitors' book and writing **Requester. Form lodged at front desk 19 Oct** in the box marked *nature of interest*, and initialling an entry nobody asked him to initial. **Aurelia Roth reads his six-word reason out loud** — *the object is out of condition* — and a second requester is in the room: **Wynne Loach, 34, night bus conductor, QA/CR/1420, *does anything here reach a person*.** The inspection is funny and dreadful: a conservation officer of eleven years writes **UNPITCHED AT REST** in the standard condition vocabulary on a form for a *fitter's lamp* and strikes it out, and says that the word was in the template before the objects were and that there are four hundred condition files upstairs with a struck-out *unpitched at rest* in them. **The Gallery holds eight objects and nine plinths and the inventory sheet on the wall says nine.** At 20:20, in the west range service corridor, he reads the box for ninety seconds with no question, no bound, no cost, no card and no witness, and the object supplies a date in a hard hand on the corner of an envelope, a **tray with a card reading 2ND POST — 3 DAYS** — so the envelope was *delivered* within three days, which nobody knew — a gate-book box, a corner of a leaf, and a leaving card: **MALLOY, J. — Grade 2 — 2,041 hrs — NIL.** At **20:22, in the loading-bay office, in a room he has spent three weeks building, thirteen adults in it of whom eleven are the meeting, plus a tape recorder, a camera and a municipal conciliation field officer**, he says it: **ninety-four on the gate; the pay run said ninety-two; and two people worked and were never entered in any book.** A field officer asks him for a name for the two and he says he does not have one and she writes *two persons, unnamed, not in any book* and reads it back and he initials it. **Hattie Bramm speaks for the first time in thirty-one years**: *we were on the gate, we were the last ones through, and then the wages came round on the Friday and the room went quiet.* She refuses, in front of two recorders, to do a sum, and gives the reason. **Wynne Loach asks him the direct question** — *can you ask it, is there a thing there, is that what you do* — and he says yes and says it is not a demon and not a saint and does not seek worship and that **its demonstrated function is a count spoken aloud where the counted can hear it**, and the fieldperson writes it on a form. The hearing is set for **Thursday 26 November at 18:00**, with the undertaking that everything said will be read out in full and kept, and a fee of **£140 payable whether or not they attend and whether or not they say anything.** Hattie will not be a witness and stays a claim. **The bell is under a seal and nobody asks it anything. No Index line.**

## Chapter 0026 — What He Wants Asked and What He Does Not

Tue 09:00 – 22:00. A school on Hall Lane telephones at ten past nine and moves a term booking from Friday 13 November to **Saturday 7 November, same time, same room**, for a one-sentence legitimate reason, and the Museum says yes in four seconds. **The Hall re-opens at 10:00** on the re-issued Annexe C, one sentence, and Mara Iles reads it in the doorway and says *a certificate is not a statement about the past; it is a statement that nobody has damaged anything since I last looked.* **Anja Ward is not told, and knows, because Mara told her on Sunday afternoon in ninety seconds in a corridor** — and when Elias tells her himself, four seconds late, in her doorway, she says: *Mara did it to me and you did it to me and one of those is a colleague and one of those is a man who has been suspended for a fortnight and has just handed the single most useful card in the building to a school on a telephone without a plan. It is not the same and it is not meant to be the same.* At half past eleven, in the loading-bay office, Mara hands over the form and says **the document that has to be in a claim file has a blank in it and the blank is in the right place**, and that she could ring him tonight and get it completed by Thursday and that is exactly why it says *may be left blank* — *it is not in the form to protect him, it is in the form to protect me from me.* At twenty past two Elias telephones **Perry Loach** for nine minutes and **takes no notes**, because a man who takes notes on a telephone call to another man in overalls has not made a call, he has made an interview. Perry wants it asked **in front of the people that was in it**, and does not want anybody asking him if he has remembered it right, and says it once and will not say it twice: *you do the checks, don't you. I've got a folder and a queue and nine years and I'm not a check. I was a fitter.* At four o'clock Elias rehearses the public consent statement in the empty Hall, four attempts, and Ivo Pell listens to all four without making a joke, and points out that Elias said *the woman in this room* to a man who works here, and that nobody will ever be able to find out who she is, and: *you have had four minutes with a bell and you have not had four minutes with her, and that is the actual bug in it, and it is not in the object.*

## Chapter 0027 — The Man in the Good Coat

Wed 09:00 – 21:00. A private hearing in Cattlemarket, Northglass, in a claim the Museum is not a party to, and **Elias Venn is not in the room and spends nine hours in a corridor with a folder he does not open.** Told entirely through the mouths of people coming out of it. **The gate book is a line in a list. The lamp is a line in a list. A fitter's lamp is plant and a bundle of wage sheets is not, and the argument comes down to an address**, and Mr Rhys of Quill & Frayle says the true and terrible thing out loud to Elias's face: *the entire three weeks of your professional life is, in the correct opinion of an honest man, a matter for the compensation section of an Act and not for a hearing at all.* **Doreen Sarn is in the building all day and refuses to be added to the witness list**, on the grounds that the moment she stands up she becomes a piece of paper, and asks to be a member of the public instead. Nineteen names and eight of them dead. At half past four a man of seventy-eight comes up the stairs with a frame and a plastic carrier bag: **Arthur Neve**, of Ravenscourt and Hale, 6 Northgate Steps, dissolved 1991, the estate's agent, with a bad chest since a pleurisy in 1969, who came because he saw the weighbridge photograph in the Sunday paper. **He has the buyer's carbon of the same eleven items**, a different hand, a different order, a line at the foot reading *received, all, subject to weight*, and — the batch's engine — **item 11, *clock, works office, stopped 05:54***, which is the number in the reading, written by a hand that did not know it was writing evidence. At ten past seven a solicitor comes out of the room and refuses him out loud: *if you give evidence, then the photograph is evidence, and then the drawer is evidence, and then the night you were in that corridor is evidence, and I am not going to sell that to anybody, including you* — and then, unprompted, in a taxi, she tells him the third thing: nineteen names and eight of the dead and the only evidence about the morning in the room has been about a wage, and a man of seventy-eight is on nobody's list. **Dael Ordway comes down the stairs at twenty past seven and will not tell him what a solicitor said to him in a corridor at twenty to four, and says it was a quantity of money and that he will say he does not remember it, and that it will not be a lie and they will both know it is not a lie.** Close: eleven things in eleven buildings and not one of them in the same room.

## Chapter 0028 — The Manifest Transfer Schedule

Thu 09:00 – 22:00. The correct document destroys the claim's last chance. **The Manifest Transfer Schedule**, 08:40, drawn up by the Registrar not from the box's label — *Sillick Mill: administrative papers, disposal* — but from the **1974 carbon in drawer 6B**, under three columns: **no opening / condition as received / date of collection 30 Oct 1974, last working day**, with eleven named lines including **3. Gate book (leaves 1–96 present)** and **10. Bundle of wage sheets, tied with tape, marked pay office.** *A receiving party who signs for a gate book cannot then say the gate book was nobody's.* Elias tells Nunn what is in the cage and Nunn refuses in two seconds: **a man who does not open a box he has signed for is a man who has signed for a box, and a man who opens a box he has signed for is a man who has opened a box**, and he is right, and Elias can only say it differently and not better. Then the thing neither of them expected: the disposition **lapses at the close of business today**, and Nunn says *Christ, that's brilliant*, and Elias says it is not brilliant, it is a clause a director wrote in a hurry at six o'clock on a Thursday morning, and it is the only good news anybody has got out of an institution in a month and it came out of a schedule. At two o'clock Mara produces **MPB/R/8841**, the answer to the request made under her own clause seven, and the load-bearing sentence is that **a bureau's remittance record is a record of the person and of money paid and is not a record of the employer** — signed **H. LOUND, Chief Clerk.** Doreen Sarn is brought into a loading bay, reads it, and rules: **on the basis of a record of that kind I will not treat a sealed item as destroyed.** And then she and Elias say the same sentence at the same second — **somebody opens the box** — and he understands that the only person legally able to is a transfer clerk who has refused, and that he cannot be it because a man who opened a box is a man who has opened a box, and Mara Iles decides it on the first floor: not in this building, not in a gallery, not on a Saturday, and not with him in the room, and **Friday 27 November, the day after the hearing, in her own front room, with a fire on.** Hattie Bramm at twenty past eight gives **six out of about a hundred and forty, nine dead, two in a home, one in a hospital**, refuses a total, and says the reason: *the day I move that list, it belongs to somebody.* Close: **a letter is a knock on a door with no knocker.**

## Chapter 0029 — Told First, in a Room, in Advance

Fri 09:26 – 22:00. **The disposition lapsed at four minutes past midnight**, nobody rang anybody, and the corridor is different: the cage is furniture, on a piece of card under a third castor.
