The letters went out on Wednesday in a plain envelope, in **Mara Iles's own person and not on the Director's letterhead**, with a reply card, four numbered paragraphs and no number in them. **The front room of the Sillick Lane branch library at 11:00 on a Friday, with the queue running in the back room at the same time, and a wall between them.** Six people come of eleven; five households produce nobody; **the Museum has instructed itself, in writing, not to find out why**, and that written refusal is the chapter's hardest sentence. **Hattie Bramm refuses to be the speaker** — *I have spoken in a room about a number four times in nine years and every one of them was because somebody who owed me money didn't want to be in the room* — and the first voice in the room is therefore a worker, and **Vera Pilling, 81, of 6 Sillick Lane, stands up at the side of the table uninvited** and says four things in ninety seconds, of which the second is the chapter: *there are two hundred and forty-odd households got a letter off that woman in the last month, and in thirty-one years I never had one, and I want it on the record, from a woman of eighty-one, that that's what this has been for me. Not the bell. Not the museum. The letter.* The two flags of Chapter 24 are read out and Hattie rules that the second one is a flag and not a fact and puts both accession numbers on the same line herself. **Mara Iles states in advance, in front of six people, that she is not here for the Director and will not give an opinion.** At four o'clock the Museum issues Statement 4 and does not mention the meeting, and cannot correct the first sentence in the *Chronicle* on Saturday morning because **it promised a room that it would not**, which is the price and the whole of it. At 21:14 Elias finds **two undelivered letters**: Doreen Sarn's, registered, signed for by a niece who is post, face down on a sideboard since Wednesday; and Cliff Sowerby's, through a box in a door at number 14, taken in by **Hattie Bramm** and carried in her coat all day and not opened. **Both are the most owed in the business and neither has been told.** Close: ninety-one lines in a notebook and a form in a safe with a blank in it and an answer that has not yet been found.

## Chapter 0030 — Thirty-Eight Children

Sat 09:00 – 19:00. The tour. The lobby has a **weighbridge poster at a nine-year-old's eye height** with no explanation, face-out, from a campaign called *Our City in Objects* in which every one of thirty-one sheets has no caption. The Hall has a clamp on the surround, an anchor card on the plinth with five lines and the fifth being **WORD: NEXT**, and a cover standing against a wall doing nothing, because a cover on a case is a cage. **Eleven seated adults, two microphones, two recorders, a camera, a press officer and a donor's office who is not going to be in the picture.** **Hattie Bramm refuses a microphone and does not refuse the room**, with a nine-year-old laptop and a folder, and gives the reason: *if I stand up there with a microphone then this morning is a thing that happened; if I sit here with a laptop then this morning is a Friday.* **Perry Loach is on the floor and is not on the rota, and there is no column in the whole of the cleaning contract in which a man can be written, and he came because he asked Mara whether Saturdays were still on.** Elias states the consent, the cost and the exit **out loud, in plain words, to fifty-one people**, and nobody moves. A girl of about nine says *somebody put a staple in it*, Mara says *yes, somebody did, about a Tuesday morning*, and the room goes very quiet, and **Elias does not turn round and is frightened of it, and it is the best thing that happens to him in the batch.** At **11:12** he asks his one question — *what is this bell for* — and a man's voice with rain in it says **eleven**. At **11:13 the anchor card is spilled by a child and the clamp goes by the case and both are on the floor before his hand is on anything**, and the reading goes wrong because the room filled faster than a five-line card could govern, and the object reaches a child. **Perry Loach puts the mop down with both hands and says "that's the shop," twice, because a thing said once in a room of fifty-one is a private incident and a thing said twice is a record: the fitters' shop, eleven men, the last time it ever rang.** Then a boy of about nine on the floor says *twelve*; a girl of eleven says *fourteen*; a boy of thirteen says *fifteen*; a girl of sixteen says *sixteen*; and then the adults on the floor say **twenty, twenty-one, twenty-two**, and a voice nobody can locate says **twenty-four**. Hattie types the run and types the gaps, crying, and will not stop. **The gap is always three, and the gap is where somebody was not on the gate and is not in any book** — thirteen, seventeen, eighteen, nineteen, twenty-three — **and the eleven-year-old and the boy of nine stop picking up the sleeve and look at the third row, where two people are sitting with a daughter between them whose names are on no sheet in the room, and neither child says it.** A producer offers money for a figure and is refused. **The number that goes into the record is 71** — the last entry on Hattie's own list, a man called Theodore Pyle at 3 Ferry Bank — and the other box gets **UNKNOWN — H.B.** **Nobody says 94, 92 or 2 in this chapter.** The bell runs for one hundred and forty seconds, the fourth and fifth notes are lost in the noise of thirty-eight people, and it does not hollow and does not return and does not explain itself. At ten past one **Ottilie Grange** reads **Annexe E** into the record: a disturbance, a stay from all handling, use, cleaning, movement and public access until a material-change inspection, *this is not a closure, the room remains open, the lights are on and nobody may touch it*, and **the dispute route is the requester, which is Elias Venn's own form of 19 October.** Anja Ward, in the doorway, does not sign it, does not argue with it, says *he is right about all of it*, and says *I do not know how to stop it.* Close: three numbers in a tape that are a man's memory of a wet morning at a gate and are now a document in three institutions and nine newspapers that he cannot alter one figure of; the two who are not on the tape in the room; two unopened letters; a form in a safe; a cage in bay 9; and a shut latched door halfway down the Quiet Wing corridor that he has not opened and is not going to.

**Costs on the page, Batch 0003:** Ch 21 a form he is not allowed to sign and a gallery shut by a correct rule; Ch 22 a wrist on a fourteen-year-old on an exclusion zone, and a hundred and forty people behind a steel door; Ch 23 a caution on a record, a paragraph six he cannot take back, and two names struck off a list in his own hand; Ch 24 the wrong object lawfully removed, a boy's radial styloid, a bell in a loading bay at one in the morning with eleven people in the room, **four seconds of a name**, and a cost he chose before he knew what it cost; Ch 25 **ninety-four, ninety-two and two said aloud in a room, on a tape, at 20:22, attributed to him in a municipal record, and a blank in a form that is the right shape**; Ch 26 an hour in an empty gallery getting a consent statement wrong three times; Ch 27 nine hours in a corridor, a witness list, and a refusal made out loud by a solicitor who is right; Ch 28 a schedule that names a gate book, a refusal by a transfer clerk who is right, and a sentence said twice by two people who were not talking to each other; Ch 29 five households that produced nobody and two letters that nobody could deliver, and a man of eighty-one who wanted a letter; Ch 30 a nosebleed and three days of headache, **a child reached**, two children looking at two people in the third row, and a stay that has no end date and no argument against it. **There is no second notebook entry and no second memory cost.** The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is not restated, not tidied, not stabilised and not referred to.

**Costs on the page, Batch 0004:** Ch 31 a correct decision that closes the one open drawer, and a systems-index line anybody can read; Ch 32 a notice he cannot execute, a building that goes quiet, and **a bell at 11:52 that he conceals from six people for four days**; Ch 33 five weeks of not asking one woman one question, and a number in a box that is not a count; Ch 34 **the pay office's own posted wage sheets for the last eight weeks of 1974, gone into a skip at eight o'clock and off the quay by 08:40, for ever, with nobody at fault**; Ch 35 **a number he has had since 25 October, disclosed in a room as a disclosure-shaped fact, after a false *no* to a question asked in the ordinary way**; Ch 36 Hattie Bramm's four minutes given to a systems technician, and Dael's sentence about him given to a transfer clerk; Ch 37 a letter not opened and a promise not kept, and a registered letter taken away unopened; Ch 38 four photographs and a statement still in two rooms, and a Director who will not sign on a spot; Ch 39 **a name on a public Board minute and a bureau form with a null in it**; Ch 40 **a determination on a public register that names a gallery and a room with children in it, a twenty-eight-day window he has told nobody about, a work authorisation with T. VENN on it, and the same sentence twice on one page of a notebook.** **There is no second notebook entry and no second memory cost. The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is untouched, unrestated, unreferred to and unstabilised.**

# Volume 01 — Batch 0004 (Chapters 31–40), "The Ledger"

Movement 4 of 5. The batch's argument is that the ledger is administrative: a wage claim is a piece of paper, and the paper has a deadline, and the deadline is set by a haulier and a bonded store clerk and nobody in this building.

## Chapter 0031 — A Lamp in a Cupboard (Tue 10 Nov, 07:00–22:00)

**Action and a clock.** The fitter's lamp, 1961/LW/51, comes back to Civic Hill on Tuesday morning with its seal intact, and a bonded-goods driver hands Elias a return form with a *Disposition* line and circles the wrong box, and he strikes *re-plinth* through and writes *stores* in the blank at the foot and initials it. It goes to cupboard 14, off the old frame store corridor, on a piece of hardboard, signed for and signed back on a key board at 07:41 in a column nobody reads. **The decision is correct and it costs the case something**: plinth 9 in the Gallery of Unnamed Hands is the only place in the world where the 1974 carbon receipt lies loose in an open drawer, and the cupboard is a better place for a lamp than a public plinth, and the open drawer is also the reason four exposures of drawer 6B exist in a police file. Ivo Pell corrects the location in the **systems index** on the ordinary Tuesday cycle, in a document anybody can walk in and read — third time he has done it, first time it has cost anybody anything — and Elias makes him change *amended* to *relocated* and add *by order of the receiving officer*, because a lawyer asking who moved a Schedule 12 object must not be answered with a suspended night guard's name. At 11:06 a bonded goods clerk at Broomhead Reach telephones: **break bulk Wednesday the eleventh at 09:40 and a paper merchant's collection on Friday the thirteenth at eight**, both off one line on one delivery sheet that reads *site contents — general, as per schedule*, and page one item four has a pencil tick and three letters, **HOLD**, in October, in pencil. *A pencil is not a hold.* She has never once known what was in that bay and will not start on a Friday. Elias writes two lines and a time and that is the instrument he has. He counts nine rooms rather than nine objects and stops halfway, on purpose, for the first time.

## Chapter 0032 — The Notice and the Quiet (Wed 11 Nov, 09:00–22:00)

**Discovery and consequence — the inversion.** Registry maintenance notice **QA/N/xx** arrives on the ordinary morning post, countersignatures empty, recommending that the object at plinth 34 be transferred to a controlled store, with a *NO ACTION REQUIRED — TICK BOX NOT TO BE COMPLETED BY THE ADDRESSEE* at the foot. **It cannot be executed, and the reason is two documents this building produced**: Annexe D (Aurelia Roth, 19 October — *under no circumstances to be removed from public view by a museum acting alone*) and Annexe E (a stay from all handling, use, cleaning, movement and public access, read into the record on 7 November by a Registry assessor with a duty). Two competent people in Northglass did their jobs on the same object nineteen days apart and neither had read the other's. Anja Ward countersigns and declines in a corridor. **Then the building goes quiet.** The Gallery door counter reads **126** where it read 491 on the Wednesday before; the level-two archive corridor yields four things in six minutes and five on the second run; the corridor outside the unlisted room is quieter than it has been in eighteen years. **At 11:52 the pressed-brass service bell beside the unlisted door rings once, half a tone flat, of something three floors away, and the repeater line is dead, so nothing comes down the wire, and nobody comes.** He is the only person in the building who hears it. He writes the time down and **tells nobody for four days** — not Ivo, not Mara, not Dael, not the Director — because a thing that has been measured in a building like this goes into a systems index on a screen in a room with a kettle in it, and because on 26 November he has to stand in front of six families and explain what a bell is. *The good news and the bad news are the same document.* No Index line. Nothing from Chapter 10 or Chapter 30 is spent. The object is not asked anything.

## Chapter 0033 — A Number in a Box (Thu 12 Nov, 08:00–23:00)

**Action and investigation.** Quay 3, Broomhead Reach, bay 4, in bond. The container was broken bulk yesterday at 09:40; the store weighs its lots on the second morning, and the seal is read out a second time by a man called **Mowbray** with a board and a marker, and four lots go on a platform scale set into the concrete and the boy's figure is written in a box with nothing above it: **482 kilograms.** A number with nothing above it, in a box at the bottom of a board, written forty times a day by a man who has never opened one, and it looks exactly like a count. Elias makes himself do the sum he is not going to do, so that he never does it by accident. **Miss Arkwright**, bonded goods clerk, thirty, six years in the sheds, courteous and completely blameless, offers the one thing a bonded clerk can offer: anything with a mark against it stays seventy-two hours if somebody with a letter of authority says so by five o'clock Thursday — and it is half past ten on Thursday, and the delivery sheet is signed off *RELEASE TO MERCHANT — GENERAL, AS PER SCHEDULE*, and a pencil is not a hold. The bound wage book for October 1974, item four, two hundred and ninety-one pages, a printed label, a new strap put on by a clerk because a rotten strap is the one thing in a bay you may not leave — and a skip is booked for Friday at eight, and nothing in this building can move either date.

**Then a street.** 12 Ferry Bank, second floor, first door, ten past one. A tablecloth, a plastic tumbler on the sill, a cardigan over a pinafore, a hardback cash book with a rubber band on it that she is not going to surrender. Elias has to ask her twice, properly, because he came in with the wrong sentence. **She says a street out loud: Quill & Frayle, Solicitors, 14 Lincoln's Lane, Irving Quay**, from a brass plate she remembers and a number she does not, and he already had the number — he has had it since the sixteenth of October. She put the envelope into the mill's out-tray on Tuesday the twenty-second of October 1974, in her own hand with a fourpenny stamp, on a tray marked *2ND POST — 3 DAYS*, and the mill took its own post down to the sorting office on Wednesday afternoons; it contained one sheet, a copy of a pay sheet, copied out of the gate book and the weekly sheet and the wage book on a Thursday night in November 1974 at her own table, on a carbon from the office. **They sold this Museum the mill's office on 7 November 1974 and the receipt in 1961/LW/44 says RECEIVED FROM Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane, and nobody in fifty years has ever asked who had written to them.** And she says the leaving card out loud so that nobody else can do it for her: **MALLOY, J. — Grade 2 — 2,041 hrs — NIL** — *that is one man's own hours on one grade on his own card, and it is not a wage and it is not a headcount, and he is not yours to do that to.* Her refusal of the cash book stands and has not moved. The meeting is at 20:22 on 26 November; there are fourteen days.

## Chapter 0034 — Four Hundred and Ninety-Four (Fri 13 Nov, 07:00–22:00)

**Action, consequence, and a real loss.** 07:12, Arkwright's dry-run call: eight o'clock, forty minutes, gone by twenty to nine, and the thing that would hold it is a letter of authority that has to be in by five o'clock on Thursday. He is on the quay at ten to eight. Four yellow skips. **Lot two — two strapped pallets of books, and with them the bound wage book with the pencil — does not go**, because a tail lift and a wheel stop and a man's four seconds, and the merchant's fortnightly collection is Tuesday, same firm, same yard. Lots one, three and four go in about two minutes: six sacks, the bundled paper, the trolley, the oddments, a bicycle, two wooden trays. **A man called Renwick, about fifty, signs once, on the tail, on a clipboard on a wheel, about eleven thousand of those a year, and has never opened one**, and when Elias asks him whether he wants to know what is in it he says *no*, and that is a professional answer from a man doing his job. He gives Elias the weighbridge docket number, because it takes four days to come out of that office and it comes out with the month on it. The van takes the gate at 08:40. **The loss is a document and not a body**: the pay office's own posted wage sheets for the last eight weeks of trading in 1974, in the pay clerk's hand, the only corroboration in this city of what was paid on the final run. It goes into a skip on a quay in Northglass at eight in the morning because a delivery sheet said *general*. Nobody breaks a rule. The merchant's own weighbridge says **494** at 10:31, up on the bay figure, because the paper was damp from three days in a bay in November, and the merchant's clerk will not think twice about it and is right not to. **The claim period is seven days from the collection: Friday 20 November.** Elias tried everything available: the clerk (no), a mobile number answered by a facilities contractor on a compound (he is not the company and has a pen for a fence), a solicitor's number (rings out), the Museum's own switchboard at 08:56 (the Director is not in until nine). He then rings Mara Iles at 09:20 and has to say the sentence *it went at twenty to eight*, and she says *do not say anything else on this telephone.* She gives him the accession number and asks for a description, and then says the second thing, which is worse and is nobody's fault: **she cannot file a claim to that paper because she cannot describe it, and the entry that would describe it is in the schedule of contents, and the schedule of contents has been empty since the eleventh of September, and it is empty because the records clerk retired in March and the post went to systems.** The notebook line that night is not about the loss. It is *to whom is this lost*, and there is no form in the world that will take it.

## Chapter 0035 — Two Lists of the Same Van (Sat 14 Nov, 09:00–23:00)

**The batch's spine.** **Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane, Irving Quay**, established 1871, unmoved, and the reason is a deed of covenant from 1889 forbidding the storage of paper more than four feet above the floor of the strong room. Open on Saturdays because property men come in on Saturdays. A room with a document camera on an arm. **Emyr Rhys**, fifty-eight, thirty-one years at the firm, the person who deals with what comes in, gives three sentences before anybody starts: the firm has the 1974 job file and it is not shut and nobody has ever asked; the clearance schedule is a client's document and he is not permitted to hand it over and will not, and the partner who could is a decent man who has said no twice; and Mrs Marsden reads documents as they are and not as they might be. **Beverley Marsden**, sixty-two, senior transcript clerk, twenty-six years, with a reading frame and a printed pro forma. **Arthur Neve**, seventy-eight, arrives at five past ten with a frame and a plastic carrier bag, having said no twice, and states before anybody starts that the carbon comes off him on the Saturday and goes back in his bag at the end: *it is my document until the twenty-sixth of November, and on the twenty-sixth it is the conciliation service's document, in a room, with a field officer in it.* His hand stays on the corner of it for two hours. The Museum's side was lifted out of drawer 6B by Mara Iles herself at 08:20 with a slip in with it, and she puts it back before she leaves. **Nobody accuses anybody.** Mrs Marsden reads both lists out, twice, in numbered order against the pro forma, and the order difference is neutralised by the reading, and three substantive differences and one figure survive: **item 3** *gate book (leaves 1–96 present)* against *gate book, with entries to 30 Oct*; **item 10** *bundle of wage sheets, tied with tape* against *bundle of wage sheets, tied with tape, marked pay office*; **item 11** *the works-office clock, stopped* against *clock, works office, stopped 05:54* — plus, on the buyer's foot only, *received, all, subject to weight.* **05:54 is six minutes to six, and Elias has had that number since the small hours of Sunday 25 October, out of a bell, in a loading bay, and he cannot say where he got it.** So he says the true thing instead of the lie, which is worse: that he has been in a room with an object from that consignment, has put his hand on it, has asked it one question out loud, that it gave him a number, that he could not tell them the number and could not tell them if he wanted to, and that the whole of it is in paragraph six of a cautioned statement in a police file in Low Ward, and he would like it minuted that he said it in a room rather than waited to be asked in one. A witness of seventy-eight says he would rather have come because of the carbon, and that a document is a thing you can put in front of a person and say *is this yours, yes or no*, and that what Elias has described is not a document. *It is either a clerical error or a decision, and nobody in the room can tell and nobody in the room wants to know yet.* **The address pays.** Mrs Marsden will say what the firm's own destruction schedule says — it is the firm's document and disclosable — and a cross-reference is written in ink beside **INCOMING — w/c 21 X 74 — 1 env. personal, office of origin, 1 — DESTROYED 1979, batch 34/74**: the envelope from 22 October 1974 went into that building and was pulped in 1979 under a schedule approved by a partner, and **it cannot be got back, and nobody's wrongdoing is in the room, and Emyr Rhys signs a cross-reference naming D. SARN as the addressee and alive and the schedule unaltered.** Then the fee ledger, which is a book and is not destroyed: **29 X 74. To attend site, Low Ward; take delivery of mill office papers, and —**. Four clauses, three of them long, the fourth two words: **take no instructions.** Eleven guineas, 1974. The carbon goes back into the plastic wallet into the carrier bag onto Arthur Neve's knees and leaves with him up six steps to a bus, because a doctor in 1971 told him not to sit in a small warm car with a man who is going to talk. At 12 Ferry Bank he is told, and she takes it as a fact about a room, and she asks for her initials to go in whatever form gets made, and not his. **No god, no reading, no supernatural event, no Index line.**

## Chapter 0036 — The Man on the Floor (Sun 15 Nov, 10:00–22:00)

**Relationship and procedural pressure.** **Ivo Pell** has booked a Registry attendance for the Gallery's re-certification — **Tuesday 24 November, 14:00, ASSESSOR (ROTA), not Roth, not Grange** — and it is on the front-desk screen, which is public, and Elias is standing at the counter, and *this time nobody has to tell him afterwards.* Ivo's joke about the weighbridge poster with no caption (*that's not the same thing*) and his work and the thing he is frightened of are all in the same conversation: four exposures in a police record, clause five, two written offers and two corridors and nothing back, and *a rota is the most frightening document in this building and there are about forty of them and I read all forty of them on Monday.* **Hattie Bramm's fourth minutes go to Ivo Pell, not to Elias.** She comes up on a Sunday because the Friday queue was the first since the seventh; she is the only member of staff on the floor; his own rule — *a person on their own is a party* — means he cannot leave. She reads the 1974 receipt standing up at plinth 9, a document from that mill she has never seen in nine years, and at about 14:00 she asks him the question nobody has asked her: **the hundred and forty are the ones she can put to a street; the ones she cannot get are the people who never had a letter, and there is no list of those and there never will be, because you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept.** She says that if anybody stands her up on the 26th she will say that, and then it will be in a file instead of a gallery, and that is the reason she has not said it for nine years. Four minutes, written on a systems sheet in ballpoint, carried down two flights to a counter. **Elias does not go and find her**, and works out why, and writes it down on the Monday and then crosses most of it out, and it is the sixth time. **Dael Ordway says the thing about Elias to Nunn, the transfer clerk, in a bay at half past four on Thursday**, word for word, and it is not said kindly and not said unkindly: *there is a man on a seven-to-three and a six-to-nine rhythm who is not on the payroll and not on the rota and who has been inside more barred rooms this month than any member of staff, and there are days when I have to write down whether he was in the building, and I have started writing it, and I do not want to be the man who has a column with his name in it* — and then, *I have got a liability position in a transcript in Northglass that I cannot carry by myself.* Nunn gives it to Elias because Elias asked him once not to improve on anybody. **The docket arithmetic**, on the back of a clipboard in a loading bay at four o'clock on a Sunday: served Wednesday 4 November, ten working days, weekends out, the day of service not counted — day one is Thursday the fifth and day ten is **Wednesday the eighteenth**; the parallel October calculation is exact (issued Thursday the fifteenth, tenth working day Thursday the twenty-ninth, dead at 00:04 on the Friday the thirtieth). So the last moment anything can be done about the Sillick box is **17:00 on Wednesday 18 November**, and at about four minutes past midnight on **Thursday the nineteenth** it is furniture for the second time in eleven weeks — and the Board sub-group sits on the Wednesday from eleven to six, and the material-change inspection is on the Thursday at eleven, and nobody has put the three dates in the same room, and doing it is the first useful thing he has done all month. A woman rings the front desk on a Sunday morning to ask whether drawer 6B is meant to be open; the message goes on a board in Nunn's office; Elias shuts the drawer himself at 17:12, in front of four people, initials his own time in the day book, and it is the first unauthorised act he has committed since 25 October.

## Chapter 0037 — The Letter (Mon 16 Nov, 10:00–22:00)

**Recovery and emotional payoff.** He goes to the laundrette at twenty past eight and tells his mother in the doorway that he is going to open the letter, because a man who says it in a doorway has made it a decision with a time on it. She says *all right* and does not look up. He sits on the bench and looks at the shelf above the boiler for about two minutes and does not get the stepladder. He counts eleven machines and notices he is counting **rooms** now and not objects, and his mother notices it and says *that's new*. A bay check at 11:20 — not opened, not to be opened before Friday 27 November at 16:00 in the addressee's own front room — and then the thing he has been not doing since 10 October: **he writes the whole of what is in the notebook, seven items, on the back of a bay-check sheet.** Four readings, four seconds of a name he cannot identify, a corridor, and item 7, *the notebook itself, a hundred and fifteen pages, nobody in this building has read it, Mara Iles has asked for it and I have not said no, and that is worse.* **12 Ferry Bank, ten past one.** He gives her the date: the envelope opens in her own front room on Friday 27 November at four o'clock, with two conciliation officers, a supervisor and a camera, and no Museum employee in it, and he will not be there. She says *that's the first thing you've said in two minutes that I believe, and I'll tell you why — everybody who's ever come in here about that envelope has wanted to be in the room.* She refuses the cash book again. She pushes the Museum's registered letter across the table — face down under a plate since 29 October — and says *take it away, and don't tell me what it says, because if you tell me what it says then I have known and my three weeks is a different three weeks* — and says she will open it on Friday night after the box has been open. And the coat pocket: *a book with one reader is a book that can lose itself, because a book with one reader is not a record, it is a habit, and the moment somebody has to prove what was in it, the habit's not evidence, it's an opinion* — and she turns it on him, because the book on her table is a list of one household and she has told nobody in nine years where the shelf is. **He does not open the letter.** He has a real reason, and it is the promise: *the right order is after I have told Mara everything*, and he has not, and item 7 is the shape of the not-telling. At ten to ten his mother gives him both halves about nine inches apart: **not doing it is the growth** — in five weeks he has done six hard things and this is the first where the hard thing was to do nothing — **and** in about eleven months she will be the Director and somebody with a clipboard will ask why a man she promoted had a private notebook for five weeks, and *the right order was after he told me everything* is not an answer anybody can use at eleven o'clock in a room with a projector. *Take it in and shut the door and give it to her, and then never do it again for the rest of your life.* No reading, no object, no supernatural event.

## Chapter 0038 — Anja Ward and the Fourth Monday (Tue 17 Nov, 09:00–23:00)

**Political and institutional pressure.** Anja Ward reads out, before she puts it in the drawer, a card in her own hand dated 20 October that she has had on her table for four weeks and has never said aloud: *E. Venn, 32. Suspended on work order 4412-S, a door 7 corridor fault he did not close. No further incident attributed to the same cause in five weeks and none likely. He has been the mechanism twice… He reports honestly and late. He is the man in the room now and not the man with the fact, and the man in the room is not safer. A person who causes the events he investigates should not be the investigator. I do not have a mechanism to say that in a document and it is the only reservation I hold.* It goes in a drawer dated the seventeenth, not the twentieth, because the reservation has four more weeks of evidence under it. **Decision under Standing Instruction 14 clause five, dated today and expressly not back-dated**: Ivo's four exposures were made in the course of a scheduled, indexed, disclosed condition survey and clause five is not complied with — not because of a distinction of professional status, and if a member of staff photographs collections material in the course of a duty the duty is the reason and the status is not a defence in any other case. **No disciplinary action. Consequent condition from 1 December: every photographic exposure of collections material by a member of staff must be countersigned at the point of making.** The Director says out loud that she has taken the only thing off him that made him safe and is giving it back as a condition, and that she would like it recorded that she knows it. Ivo asks her to be told the door thing was him and not the wall, and says the sensible thing is that the thing he did was right and he has been sick about it and now he has been given permission and **he does not want the permission**, and that this is the sentence his sister says about her chest. **The review is set for Wednesday 25 November, 09:25**, because the notice on the noticeboard says *fourth Monday* and a document is corrected and a person is not, and because on Thursday the twenty-sixth six households are going to be in a room and she would like a file with something in it from the Wednesday. **Clause six goes to the Director at last**, by Mara, in a doorway, in ninety seconds, on a printed Museum form with a print run of four hundred: *disclosure of documentary material held elsewhere*, with a box for *why it has not previously been disclosed to this Museum* — which is **because I was not asked** — and a box for *action requested* — **request the document into a Museum file under the date of this request** — because a request does not need a number and a request does not need a clause. Anja signs nothing on the spot and gives a date: Friday, or a written reason on Friday. The two packages do not move and the problem is not solved, and the chapter says so in the voice of a man who has run out of instruments: he has run out of forms, the only two moves left are on the Thursday and on the twenty-sixth, neither of them is his, and there are a hundred and fifteen pages in a coat pocket that nobody in the world has read.

## Chapter 0039 — Item Five (Wed 18 Nov, 09:00–22:00)

**Investigation and the public exposure.** The Board's schedule of contents sub-group sits from eleven until about six. Item five is the Sillick Mill entries, and the minute note under it — put there by **Dael Ordway on 9 October** — says the material is *unavailable since 11 September* because a proposed disposition is before the standing instruction. **At 17:00 that afternoon the last of those dispositions runs out, and nobody on the second floor knows it, and nobody on the second floor can know it, because there is no box on the paper for *the reason the last box stopped being a live possibility*.** Nunn says so on the telephone at 16:20 and says it is the best sentence anybody has produced about this building in five weeks. The minute goes down to the front desk at half past six and Elias reads it standing at a public counter at ten past seven. **A trustee — Mrs Angela Vessey, sixty-four, since 2019, with a right of access to Board papers — has read the statement of 19 October that clause six put in the Board Secretary's file and has asked whether a person named in it appears in the Museum's records.** The Registrar answers. **The name is Wendel Case, grade 2 porter, 1994 to 2012**, at entry 1961/LW/52 (b), on a staff file in part illegible and in part amended, on the bureau's deductions for 1994, on a card in a gallery that was cut, and on a list of eleven names written and signed in a loading bay on 29 October. **Mara Iles reads it out because she wrote it and because she will not have it read out by a trustee**: *I wrote it on the twenty-ninth of October at about five o'clock in the afternoon at a table in a loading bay, and I did it because a transfer schedule that does not name a gate book is a schedule that lets a company say the gate book was nobody's, and I would do that again on a Saturday in the conservation office, on my own, in a hurry, with nobody asking me to.* **Nobody in that sentence is a villain and every step of it is a correct procedure correctly applied.** The cost of the exposure is a request to the Municipal Payroll Bureau with **two questions on one form**, and H. Lound, who has never heard of the building, is going to have to answer the second one with nothing, and sign it, and the nothing goes in a Museum file with the Museum's request number on it. **Item four is the Board's real business and nobody says the word.** On a projector, at A3, because the original is in a case on a plinth: **QUIET ACCORD — SCHEDULE 12. CARRIED PATTERNS. Objects and records carrying a set form held in common use. ANNEXE D (PERMANENT). A PATTERN REQUIRING CONTINUED OBSERVANCE. TO BE DISPLAYED.** A trustee of sixty-four reads the top line out loud because she reads headings. Anja Ward: not a religious classification; the oldest in the city; four per cent of what we hold; an object that carries a repeated human act; a hand bell, no maker's mark, no devotional content, cast 1902. Nine of them displayed across three galleries on open plinths, and the rope line is the whole of the provision. And *who is observing it?* — **Whoever is in the room. That is the mechanism. It has been the mechanism for two hundred years in this city and it is the reason the buildings are free, and it is not a metaphor… and that is why I would not let anybody use the word in a minute, because the word would be accurate, and a minute is a public document.** The Hall certificate stands, condition four is satisfied because the object is not out of its last certified condition, no school group, no party without a collections pass, and the minute's last line — asked for by the Director and not debated — records **that the Annexe E stay is a stricter instrument than her own condition four, and that the Museum's own file is the only place that is written down.** Elias does not write the word down and cannot account for that, and it is the only thing in five weeks he does not do.

## Chapter 0040 — The Box in the Second Envelope (Thu 19 Nov, 08:00–23:00)

**The batch turn: the inspection, the determination, and the origin.** The docket died at 00:04 that morning — *did not travel. No removal took place* — and the cage has been furniture since 1 November and will be furniture at Christmas. **A box in bay 9 stopped being a live possibility at four minutes past midnight and an object on plinth 34 is inspected at eleven the same morning, and the only person in the city with both facts in one notebook is a suspended night guard who is not allowed in one of the two rooms they are in.** The Hall of Former Faiths is open and about thirty people are in it, because a stay is not a closure. Present at the case: **Aurelia Roth** (Schedule 12) and **Ottilie Grange** (second assessor), both of whom have never seen a piece of the case's evidence and neither of whom asked to; M. Iles, registrar, not signing as an officer; S. Spence, metals; A. Ward, Director; I. Pell, systems, in the north door; and **Elias Venn, as a member of the public, ref QA/CR/1187, signed in at 10:26, in a coat, on the public side of the rope.** The assessors work for forty minutes without speaking. Nobody opens the case, because the assessor's inspection is not handling. **The material-change question is asked of the object and answered of the object: no material change.** The seal is intact, there has been no handling, use, cleaning, movement or public access, Annexe D stands, Annexe E stands. And then **box 4(d)**, headed *other observations material to the determination*, the same pen: on 7 November this object was reported as having done something, and the schedule does not list that as a characteristic of an object, and it is the only thing in the file that has happened in six weeks. **Aurelia Roth's determination: 4(c) is empty, and the plain reading of Annexe E would lift the stay, and it is not available** — *if a stay lifted on a determination of no material change, then every stayed object in this city would lift the first time an inspector looked at it and found it stable. I am not going to improve that clause. The Registry does not write the schedule and there are four hundred copies of it in four hundred buildings and every one of them has a 4(d) on it that somebody put there in about 1840 because they needed somewhere to put the true things that would not fit in the other boxes.* **The stay stands. Nobody is a villain. Annexe E is stricter than the Director's condition four, and Roth says so, and a public gallery is full of people who do not know any of this is happening.** She separates the appeal route from the material-change route so that Chapter 30 and Chapter 40 do not contradict each other. **Elias uses his one public question at ten past one, after a school group has come and gone: *can a member of the public see the determination?*** Yes — the public register, Friday morning, no charge, and the number can be quoted, **and the route to it is his, because he asked for an inspection at a front desk in October with six words on a form, and a public right that cannot be exercised in public is not a right, it is a procedure.** *You are the reason a determination about that object is going to be on a public register in this city in the morning. I am sorry about that.* And then she gives him the one dated thing that is in his hands, in a public gallery, to six people, and will not send it: **Annexe E's dispute route does not close at a determination. A requester may put a further question to the Registry, in the form of a request, and the office will list it, and if it is listed it will be decided on a stated day with a stated assessor in the room. The form is the same form you used in October. There is a box on it for a period. The period is twenty-eight days from the date of determination, and today is the nineteenth of November, and the last day is the seventeenth of December.** She will not help him write the question, because the moment the Registry helps, it is the Registry that asked whether an object reaches a person, and it never will be. She has written the date on the back of her own copy in pencil and will not send it, and says that if he does not use it she will regard that as the correct outcome. **The number of people who can be hurt goes up and does not go down: a public register, a registration number, a gallery, a date, and a room with children in it.**

**And the origin.** At 16:32 he is sent to the security room — the one room in this building he is permitted to be in, with the loading bay, the loading-bay office and the service lane — because an assessor has asked for the card-access record of the Hall of Former Faiths, and the access system is administered from a terminal with a red lever-arch file beside it labelled **CARD ACCESS — 2006 REORGANISATION / QUIET WING**. The instruction is **06/QW/11, 11 October 2006**, with a typed name at the foot that is on no list of anybody who has ever worked here, and it has an attachment envelope stapned inside it marked **ATTACHMENTS — DO NOT SEPARATE** that has been there for eighteen years. He has never opened it and does not now, and the reason he does not is in a corridor in a chapter in October and in about four seconds standing at a terminal. The corner of the envelope has lifted. There are two documents inside. One is a folded plan of the Quiet Wing corridor with a door drawn on it and, in pencil, in a hand that is not his: **already latched when I came.** The other is a printed form:

> **WORK AUTHORISATION — 4417/06** *(Protective works. Contract 4417.)*
> **Location:** Quiet Wing corridor, level 4, west range. **Item:** east end — door and lobby.
> **Scope:** make good, seal, and re-hang. Fit secondary latch to corridor side. **Leave the primary latch engaged.**
> **Also:** re-site the ward repeater bracket at half-landing. Existing bracket to remain in position and not to be removed.
> **Date of authorisation:** 2 November 2006. **Authorised by:** *T. VENN.*
> **Countersigned:** ☐ **not countersigned. Job complete:** ☐ **not completed.**
> and, in a second pen, a later date: **Closed 3 Nov 06. Bracket not re-sited. Lamp not returned. Left as found. R.M.J.**

**He does not prove anything and he cannot.** The instruction is dated 11 October 2006 and the authorisation is dated **2 November 2006**, three weeks later, and nobody can say why. **T. VENN is on no list of anybody who has ever worked here and there is no T. VENN on the bureau, and the two letters at the front of the signature are his own and he worked that out in October and has told nobody, and the chapter now says plainly that it gets him nothing: a man who has found his own initials on a form has not found the hand that wrote them. An earlier version went on to name the Director as the only person alive who could have signed it; that was a non-sequitur and it is cut.** **And the fourteen-year-old boy on the site in Chapter 24 is not this fourteen-year-old and is not him** — that boy was on a work-experience exclusion zone at about twenty-five past one in the afternoon on **24 October**, on a site that was being cleared, with a college tutor, a site induction record and a foreman called Upton, and he got a fracture of the radial styloid, and nobody is to blame, and that boy is a different fourteen-year-old on a different site in a different decade and has nothing whatever to do with a bell. **This is the fourteen-year-old, and the fourteen-year-old has been in the room since the tenth of October, and the man has been the fourteen-year-old all along and not a man with a talent, and the difference between those two sentences is the difference between a thing you have and a thing that happened to you.** He puts the authorisation back, puts the file back, signs the shelf's own sheet at **16:52** and does not write on it what he took out. He tells nobody for two days, and writes down two reasons, and crosses out four fifths of the first, and leaves the second: *two days because if I say it on Thursday then on Friday I have said it, and there is no way of knowing afterwards whether I said it because of the document or because of the door.* **And at about 20:20, at a table in a flat in Low Ward, he takes out the notebook to write down what a piece of paper in a buff envelope said, because that is the only instrument he has ever had — and the same sentence is on the page twice.** In his own hand, in the ink and the pressure and the lean he could not improve on in his life, copied in on Monday the sixteenth at 11:20 from a bay check he wrote standing up with a clipboard on his knee; and beneath it, in a rounder and lighter hand with less pressure and a slant the other way, the same sentence word for word, dated *Mon 16 Nov, 11:20*. Nothing is wrong with either. Nothing is missing from either and nothing is extra, both halves are complete, neither half refers to the other, and a clean page with one sentence on it in his own hand does not account for the same sentence in another hand on the same page on the same day. **He is not frightened, and he should be.** There is no second notebook entry, no memory cost, no name gone, no headache, no blood. The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is untouched. The crack in the bell's shoulder is still open under the staple and is not going to heal.

### The week-four day map, as drafted

| Day | Ch | Clock | What fixes it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Tue 10 Nov** | 31 | 07:00 – 22:00 | Lamp returned, seal intact, stores, not re-plinthed. Ivo's Tuesday systems-index line. Arkwright telephones 11:41: break bulk Wed 11th 09:40, paper merchant Fri 13th 08:00. |
| **Wed 11 Nov** | 32 | **07:00** – 22:00 | **QA/N/xx** cannot be executed (Annexe D + E). The building goes quiet, and he goes looking for the thing that would make his own theory false and finds one. **11:52** the pressed-brass service bell rings once, half a tone flat, and nobody came; the bay until 12:04, the half landing from 12:15, the entry written there. He tells nobody for four days. |
| **Thu 12 Nov** | 33 | 08:00 – 23:00 | Break bulk was the previous morning; **lot weighing 09:58, 482 kg**. Pencil tick on item four. **12 Ferry Bank, 10:10** — the address: Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane. |
| **Fri 13 Nov** | 34 | 07:00 – 23:00 | 07:12 dry-run call. 07:50 quay. **08:00 collection, 08:22 signed, 08:40 off the quay.** 08:44 the mobile; 08:56 the switchboard; 09:20 Mara; 11:20 Arkwright again, **494 kg** at 10:31 on the yard. Claim period closes **Fri 20 Nov**. |
| **Sat 14 Nov** | 35 | **08:20** – 23:00 | **The carbon comes out of drawer 6B at 08:20 and goes back into it at about four o'clock with the slip in with it.** **Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane.** Both carbons read aloud, twice. Three differences and one figure. The destruction schedule, the cross-reference, the fee ledger, **take no instructions**. The carbon leaves with Neve. 12 Ferry Bank at half past one. |
| **Sun 15 Nov** | 36 | 10:00 – 22:00 | Ivo's Registry booking on the public screen. **Hattie Bramm's four minutes, 14:00, to Ivo.** Nunn and Dael at 16:30. **The ten-working-day arithmetic on a clipboard.** 17:12 drawer 6B shut, initialled in the day book. |
| **Mon 16 Nov** | 37 | **08:20** – 22:00 | **The laundrette at 08:20.** Bay check 11:20. The seven-line inventory of the notebook. **12 Ferry Bank, 13:10** — the date of 27 November, the letter pushed across the table, the coat pocket. **The laundrette, 20:20–22:00** — and the letter does not move. |
| **Tue 17 Nov** | 38 | 09:00 – 23:00 | Anja's card read out. **Clause five decided in writing**, dated today and not back-dated. **The review set for Wed 25 Nov, 09:25.** Clause six goes to the Director in a doorway, on a form, with a date of Friday. |
| **Wed 18 Nov** | 39 | **07:00** – 22:00 | **The paper on the board from seven.** Sub-group 11:00–18:00. **17:00 the last disposition runs out and nobody upstairs knows.** The minute down at 18:30, read at a counter at 19:10. **Wendel Case on the page.** Item four, the Schedule 12 heading, *whoever is in the room.* |
| **Thu 19 Nov** | 40 | 08:00 – 23:00 | The docket died 00:04. **Material-change inspection 11:00; determination about 12:20; Elias's one question 13:10.** A coach party of adults at twelve, a notice from Wednesday on the board, and no children in the building. **The twenty-eight days end Thursday 17 December.** **T. VENN, work authorisation 4417/06, dated 2 November 2006, at 16:52.** The two sentences on one page, at about 20:20. |

### Costs on the page, Batch 0004

Ch 31 a correct decision that puts the only loose copy of the 1974 carbon in a cupboard, and a systems-index line that anybody can read; Ch 32 a notice that cannot be executed, a building that goes quiet, and a bell he hears and tells nobody about for four days; Ch 33 five weeks of not asking a woman one question, and a number in a box that is a weight and not a count; Ch 34 **the pay office's own posted wage sheets for the last eight weeks of 1974, gone into a skip at eight in the morning and off the quay by 08:40, for ever, with nobody at fault**; Ch 35 a number he has had since a loading bay and cannot account for, said in a room as a disclosure-shaped fact that is worse than a lie; Ch 36 a sentence that went to a systems technician because he was the only person on the floor, and two names struck off a list in October still not explained; Ch 37 a letter not opened, a promise not kept for eleven days yet, and a registered letter taken away unopened; Ch 38 four photographs and a four-hundred-word statement still in two files in two rooms, and a Director who will not sign on a spot; Ch 39 **a name on a public minute and a Board pack on the 24th, and a bureau's remittance file that has to be completed twice**; Ch 40 **a determination on a public register that names a gallery and a date and a room with children in it, and a twenty-eight-day period that expires on 17 December and that nobody will send him.** **There is no second notebook entry and no second memory cost. The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is untouched, unrestated, unreferred to and unstabilised, and the first major memory-displacement cost remains reserved for Volume 03.**

---

## Chapter 0041 — The Line in the Gate Book

**The two days, paid, and the night.** **Saturday 21 and Sunday 22 November are a closed block and not two chapters.** He does the letters, goes to the laundrette at ten and does not get the stepladder, stands in the Hall of Former Faiths for nine minutes on a Saturday afternoon as a member of the public, reads a receipt for a thing in a safe in Irving Quay without opening the safe, and stands outside number 14 Sillick Lane at six in the evening and does not knock. **The eighth time.** Two reasons are on the page and four fifths of the first is struck out, and a third reason is added on the Saturday night that is not about the document at all: *I have found out that I can hold a thing for two days. I cannot hold it for a third. That is not a moral position. That is a length of time.* **He telephones Mara Iles on Sunday at nine and she says yes in half a second.** **Monday 23 November, 08:35, the conservation office, the door shut.** He gives her the file, the shelf, the instruction number, the envelope, the corner. **She goes and fetches it herself and it is nine minutes and it is the only restricted file in the building a suspended night guard may open.** Her four questions are all about the document. **She does not say *is that your father*.** And then he tears out **the fourth page from the end** — the page with the sentence in two hands — writes the date at the top and *given to M. Iles, Mon 23 Nov 2024* under it, and puts it on her desk square with the writing uppermost, **and she writes a receipt on the back of it and dates it and initials it and puts it in a drawer and does not ask what it means.** He keeps the other hundred and fourteen pages and gives a date for them.

**The lawful door.** **ANJA WARD'S ATTENDANCE AT A NON-MUSEUM SITE — AUTHORISATION (ONE OCCASION)**, one sheet, no number, five boxes, hand-written, given at a public counter. Box 4 wants a counter-signature by a person who is not an officer or employee of the Museum and **the Director ticks it herself**, on the stated ground that she has never in nineteen years been an officer or an employee. Box 3 says, in twenty-eight load-bearing words, *currently not in the employ of this Museum in any grade.* Box 2 is *to obtain a description of site material cleared in October 2024 for the purposes of the schedule of collections* and **not** *evidence in a wage claim*, and she says why in ninety seconds: because if it says that, a claims manager in another city asks her why a museum sent a night officer onto a private site at night, and the answer would be true and would be the worst sentence in the file. **Dael Ordway's one-page risk assessment, hazards written before the site is visited, line three: *cast-iron weighbridge, 18 in. hump, kerb at side. Trip and fall hazard* — and he says in the car that he is not going to cross it out, because a form without the hump on it is a false cause on a form, and he has signed enough of those.**

**The site.** A fence, a compound, a cabin, a gate on a track with a chain and a padlock, a hardstanding, a plate weighbridge with an eighteen-inch crown and a kerb where the concrete was poured badly, and a gate hut with a light in it. **The weighbridge in the poster in the lobby is not this weighbridge and has not been since 1998, and Dael Ordway has known that since the fourth of November and has done nothing about it, because a caption is a caption: *if a caption is a lie then a card is a lie, and a card is what we put the object on.***

**The gate book is the door.** The custodian asks whether to enter a job or a name, and Elias says a name, and two names go in a line with a vehicle and no job, **and Dael Ordway reads the line and says that it wants a person and it has got one and he is not going to be precious about it.** **The clearance book is the thing they get.** A canvas-backed day book, one line per load, and the line for 23 October says the date, the day, a weight, a seal and a destination and the word *balanced*, and *balanced* is a merchant's word for the fortnightly collection. **A description, not of a thing but of a consignment — and it is what the claim wanted and what the schedule of contents wanted, and it had been on a shelf above a kettle for eleven weeks.** They did not get the ledger and they did not get the schedule of the mill office, which is in a strong room under a pavement at fourteen Lincoln's Lane on a shelf at four feet because of a covenant of 1889 and which Emyr Rhys has declined to produce twice. **The other line that matters: *22 OCT — THU. Gate hut to compound. 1 crate. 58 kg. Site compound.*** The crate is thirty yards away and has been since the twenty-second, and the man who moved it has never once been in the corner of that hut where the desk was, and *that isn't a crime, that's a crate.*

**The injury, and nobody is to blame.** Dael Ordway goes over the hump at about five to one in the morning, puts his left hand out on wet iron, and goes down on his side. **It did not sound like a snap. It sounded like a boot coming off a stair.** X-ray at 02:20: **left clavicle, mid-shaft, displaced**, *following a fall from standing height onto a wet surface in an outdoor compound*, no admission, sling, district clinic 8 December. **And it is nobody's fault, and that is four separate true things: the hump is in the hazard line a supervisor wrote before anybody went; the concrete was wet because it was November; the first hazard line says *unlit external area, single lamp at gate*; and he said *two minutes* and then went over a hump he had been looking at for half an hour.** In the back of a car at ten to four he says *I am not your friend*, that he does not think the object on plinth thirty-four is a bell, and that he would put his name in a gate book beside his any night of the week, and could not say why both are true.

**And at 07:25 on Tuesday the twenty-fourth, on the front desk telephone, Miss Arkwright tells him four sentences: the item is not yours, you cannot sign for it, come on Wednesday with a person and not with a letter off a director.** He writes one line on a hospital parking ticket: *it has to come out with a person, and the person has to be somebody this building cannot put a hold on, and I have known who that is since about ten past seven this morning and I have not said it out loud, and I am not going to write it down either.*

## Chapter 0042 — Seventy-Two Hours

**The hold, and the person with the headset.** **Reference BQ4/2511/L2/04, raised 09:09 on Tuesday the twenty-fourth, seventy-two hours, referred upwards after, lapsing 09:09 on Friday the twenty-seventh.** She is blunt about the claim: it was due in writing to the consignor by **five o'clock on Friday the twentieth** and it was not made and could not have been, and the four hundred and ninety-four kilos is at a pulp mill, and *the only person in this business who lost anything on Friday the thirteenth of November is you, and you did it standing on a hardstanding at twenty to nine in the morning with a notebook, and I told you at half past seven that it would not work and I was right and it was not anybody's fault.* And then the sentence that is the whole of the second half: **on Friday the thirteenth a registrar could not describe her own collection, and on Monday the twenty-third a night officer with a broken man in the back of the car got a description out of a weighbridge hut, and the two facts are nine days apart and they are the same fact.**

**The disclosure form does the work nobody asked it to do.** Two pages, eight boxes, no number, no clause, from a print run of four hundred that goes out with compliment slips. **Box 5: *because nobody had asked me, and because until twenty-two minutes past eight last night this Museum did not know that it could not describe a consignment, and a Museum that cannot describe a consignment cannot prove that a consignment ever existed.*** The Director signs at **11:04 on Tuesday the twenty-fourth**, and on the Friday the twentieth she signed the *first* of the two disclosure requests — the four hundred words of the fourteenth of October — **thirty-seven days** late and wrote two lines saying so and refused to have the date taken off the request, and Anja Ward's whole lesson lands: **a document in this building is worth what the route is worth and not what the document is worth.** The schedule of collections gets its **first entry since the eleventh of September** and the entry is about something that is not the Museum's. And the Director tells him, in a corridor, fast, in order of risk, that the item is not the Museum's and the Museum cannot take delivery, sign, indemnify or undertake — and then, in one sentence, refuses to say the name: **the shape of the answer is that this Museum has exactly one category of person who is not on anybody's payroll and is not on any rota and does not appear on any contract return of this building's, and cannot be instructed. (That category is the cleaning contract, and it is not Elias Venn, who is the ninth entry in the fixed-positions file of 16 September and whose hours the municipal bureau administers at M.Q.S. 4. The review pass removed four sentences across Chapters 42, 43, 44 and 47 that had him asserting the opposite.)**

**Perry Loach says no in a doorway on the Tuesday and yes on the Wednesday, and the reason he gives is the best thing in the chapter.** *I said no because it was Tuesday and you were in a doorway and it was a favour. That's all that was in it. There's no dignity in it.* And: *I thought: they've had to make a piece of paper to give me a job they could have given me in a corridor in October. And that's a daft thing to think, and I thought it anyway, and it's true, and it's not about you. You never asked me for anything, not once, in six weeks.* And what he asks for is **a piece of paper with his name on it and a time and a box at the bottom where he can write *agreed***, and the reason is that in two years somebody will say *Loach was there* and he will have to say *was I?* Mara Iles builds the form in four minutes and **leaves the grade box empty on purpose** and puts in the file that she did.

**12 Ferry Bank, ten past one, and four refusals plus one instruction.** Doreen Sarn will not receive the book at the flat, in the hall or in a corridor, *because it is not mine — the book is a mill's book, a book is not a person's book, and a person in a hall cannot be handed a firm's book by a firm that has a fence and a compound and a telephone nobody picks up.* She will not sign anything. She will not read the book out. **And she names who will sign the receipt: Hattie Bramm, because she has got standing and she has got a memory, and a person with a memory is a person who can be disputed, and I have been disputed for fifty years and I am tired of winning.** And then the instruction: **no case, no plinth, no rope, no card. A trestle table, and about seventy people walk round it, and touch it if they want to, because it is a wage book and not a relic, and if the first thing that happens to it in a public room is that it goes behind glass with a card on it then we will have spent fifty years and one broken collarbone to put a lie on a table.** And she tells him to write down the moment he wants to be in the room on Friday afternoon, *because if that man had been in the room then the first account of what was in it would have been his, and it would be true, and it would be a document with a Museum in it.*

**The assessor nobody has met certifies the Gallery of Unnamed Hands, 14:00 to 16:30.** She counts eight objects against a wall sheet dated 1988 that says nine, and plinth 9 is bare with a card, and drawer 6B was shut on a Sunday by a suspended night guard's own initials in the Museum's own day book — **and there is no authorising officer, and she records *no authorising officer identified* in box three and writes the word *unauthorised* nowhere at all, and then says: *I have been in this job twenty-two years and I have never been in a building where I could get the two to agree, and it is not because anybody here is dishonest. It is because every single person in this room is telling the truth about a different piece of it.*** **The laundrette at twenty past nine, and the shelf, and the stepladder he does not get.** And the Board pack goes up at ten past eight, and the line on the Director's letter from Friday is the one that breaks him in a corridor for four minutes: *six days late. I did not do it in six days because in six days I read a determination on a public register that named a gallery and a date and a room with children in it.*

## Chapter 0043 — Nine Sentences

**Anja Ward's chapter, and the Board takes forty-one pages and item two is nineteen of them.** Elias is brought in **for item two only**, on a chair against the wall with the door behind him, at the Chair's discretion and not the Director's request, and Mrs Vessey says *you will not have a copy of this minute, because a copy of this minute is a document about a meeting you were not invited to, and I have been a trustee of this institution for five years and I have never seen a suspended member of staff given the minutes of a Board, and I am not starting at ten o'clock on a Tuesday with a bell in the building.* Hattie Bramm's letter of the twentieth is read out in full, including the paragraph nobody wanted: *I am asking for whatever the Museum's own paper says, if it is wrong, that the wrongness be said out loud in the same room, by the Museum, and not in a leaflet.* And the sixth: *I am asking for the list of who is in the room. And I am asking that the Museum does not decide it.*

**And then the Director stands up and reads nine sentences off her own paper without looking at it, and the ninth one is the price.** The Annexe E stay is stricter than her condition four and must stay satisfied. **No child in that room.** Eighty people, the families' list, not amendable, a name against every seat, a form with a right to leave. **No figure stated by any member of Museum staff.** A workers' panel the Museum may not amend and may not add to, with the Museum's own card still underneath it. One press line containing no religious word. The Museum pays, indemnifies attendance costs, **does not settle and does not say that it will not settle**. And: **the Museum's own 1974 acquisition record goes on the table in the room, with its date, and it says that this Museum bought that mill office on the seventh of November 1974 and did not pay a disposal fee.** She says the cost in one sentence — four days from the request and three from the room and both too short; a room a woman wants and is not on a list of eighty does not enter; the list of eighty is not the list of everybody; and our own 1974 receipt goes on a table in public.

**Mrs Vessey, in order, and the second one is the one that matters.** *Every one of the nine is something I would have written.* Then: **condition five is not a condition, condition five is a surrender, and you have dressed it as a condition because a surrender in a minute is a resignation, and you have been a director for nineteen years — and I want it minuted that I said to you *that is a surrender* and that you said yes.** And Anja Ward says yes.

**The second trustee's draft release is refused in three sentences and put in an envelope marked *not executed*.** *That sentence is a release from people who were short-paid for thirty-one years. It costs this Museum nothing. It is worth a stamp. And in four years somebody will stand up in a room and say *the Museum got them to sign something*, and it will be true, and there will be a document, and I will have spent three conditions buying the appearance of a process and sold the only thing I actually had.*

**And then the Chair asks him one question** — *why on earth should this Museum do this?* — and he answers the question that was asked and not a larger one: a bronze bell on a plinth under a card with a number on it that is not a Museum accession number, the card cut, the erasure partial and on a public register since Friday; nine Schedule 12 objects on open plinths and a rope line that is not a safety measure but a mechanism, whoever is in the room, two hundred years, the reason the buildings are free; **so either the object on plinth thirty-four is not a carried pattern, or a rope line is not a provision — and neither is my decision, and neither is this Board's, and the Registry has said in a determination this week that the schedule has no threshold in it for the number or age of the people in a room, and that is not an oversight, that is the answer.** And the last part: fifty-one people, thirty-eight of them children, one of them finishing a sentence a man in his early sixties started, and nobody stopping it, **including the woman who wrote the request, who had a word that would have stopped it and would not use it because using it would have stopped the count.** She writes it in as *an answer and not a statement, because he was not asked for a statement.* And the Chair does not ask the second half of her own question, *because I do not want to know the answer on a Wednesday.* **In the corridor afterwards she says the one thing that was not procedure: nineteen years of opening no doors, and on Friday morning she is going to open one in public with a minute about it, and four of the nine people who will be unhappy about it are on the Board and one of them is her.** He writes the nine conditions down verbatim without full stops, because full stops invite improvements.

## Chapter 0044 — Eleven Minutes Past Eleven, and Six O'Clock

**Thursday the twenty-sixth of November, two scenes, and neither of them is a miracle.** **11:20 at Broomhead Reach.** Hallowgate's letter of Wednesday does the work, and the fourth paragraph is the one that made it work. Miss Arkwright, twice, to two people: **there is no indemnity in this, and I have not asked for one, because this store does not sell the goods and does not own the goods and does not care what the goods are — it sells a guarantee that nothing leaves without a piece of paper.** The release line: **item four, one off, bound, sleeve and tie as received, released to carrier P. LOACH, no employer of record, time out 11:20.** The label under the sleeve says **WAGES, PAY OFFICE**, and under it a month and a year, and **1974, OCTOBER**, and that is a label and not a revelation. **Perry Loach puts it in a plastic bag, then takes it out and uses the new strap instead because the strap was hers and she had said a rotten strap is the one thing in a bay you are not allowed to let go of.** He takes the 06:14 out of Northglass alone, waits in a lobby on a bench for the loading bay, will not let Elias Venn come in with him, signs the form at the form's box, and puts the bag on a trestle and steps back four feet and says: *it's not a museum object. It's a wage book… and every single one of them had a reason.* **He signs it there and he will not be two minutes late for a grid that has his hours on it and no name on it.**

**18:00, a hired room above a shop in Ferry Bank, about thirty-one people, a form, a fee, and a ruling that stops the arithmetic before anybody can do it.** Field Officer Marsh reads the rule out and then makes her own: **no figure is to be stated in this room in the same breath as another figure from the same source, and no one is to state a difference, and I will rule against it if it is offered — a difference is an adjudication, and I do not adjudicate, and if you want the difference then you want it in front of a judge.** The fee of £140 is paid at the door by **Hattie Bramm**, in notes, and Marsh gives the reason out loud in the doorway because she always does: *it is for the people who come and then can't speak when they're asked to, and go home, and are not thanked.*

**Arthur Neve, seventy-eight, at the front because he asked to be**, reads his own carbon out and puts it on the table, having said three times that it is his document until the twenty-sixth and the conciliation service's after. Item three, item ten, item eleven, and *received, all, subject to weight* on the buyer's foot alone. **And he says: *the clerk has certified the comparison and she says she does not certify that either document is correct, which is the first sentence anybody has said to me since October that I did not expect.*** Two documents go into the bundle in the morning and neither is given to the room: **Lound's null** — *was this person employed by anybody? No response available from this office* — which Marsh reads twice, once as written and once with the second question spoken aloud, *because the second question is in the file and the answer to it is not, and both of those are facts, and only one of them is a thing that a person has to answer for*; and **the certified comparison, of which neither copy is of the carbon**; and the occupation form, uncompleted, index saying so.

**And then, at nine minutes past six, Elias Venn states the loss of the document, knowing exactly what it costs.** Four skips, twenty to nine, *site contents — general, as per schedule*, four hundred and ninety-four kilograms at a merchant's weighbridge at half past ten, pulp by the twelfth. A clerk who read a sheet and weighed four lots. Renwick, who signs eleven thousand a year and has never opened one. **And the sentence that is the centre of it: there was a procedure for stopping it, and the procedure was a letter of authority from a person with a claim on the line, and there was no such letter, because at ten to nine that morning the Museum's registrar could not describe the consignment, and the entry that would have made the description checkable is in a schedule of contents that has been empty since the eleventh of September.** Mara Iles asks for the claim period into the record in her own name. **And he says the thing that costs: *if I stand here and say the corroboration is gone, then the only thing left in this room that supports the number is me, and I am not a document. And I would rather say it than have it found, because it is in the sack list and the weighbridge docket and the delivery sheet, and about eleven people in this building could find it, and if one of them finds it after Friday then everything anybody said in this room was true for six weeks for the wrong reason.*** Vera Pilling, eighty-one, from the second row: *Well, that's the first honest thing anybody's said to me since September.*

**Hattie Bramm says the thing about the people who never had a letter, from the back, in her own voice, unbidden, and is not a witness.** **And then Elias Venn stands up in an aisle that is not where you stand and asks Perry Loach the only question anybody has asked him in six weeks — not what happened, but *what did they say to you, the words* — and Perry Loach says *I've got the words* and does not say them, and asks for it to be minuted that he was asked, and by whom, and at what time, and by a man who is not a member of staff and cannot be instructed about it.** And on the pavement, in the rain, Doreen Sarn says four words: **third row, on the left, two women and a girl between them.** He has known for six weeks. He has not asked. She says *good*, gets on a bus, and it is the ninth time, and the only one of the nine that cost nothing.

## Chapter 0045 — The Card

**No stagecraft, and the chapter proves it by being a list.** The nine conditions as work, pinned inside the loading-bay-office door: the object not opened, handled, cleaned, moved or approached, **the cover is not put on it** because a cover on a case is a cage; eighty; the families' list; no figure from Museum staff; the panel; one five-word press line that is accurate and about which nobody in the building can decide whether it is good; and a trestle six feet from the rope with the book on it in its sleeve and **nothing under the book and no case and no rope and no plinth and no card.**

**The panel is the only object in the volume that is designed, and Hattie Bramm would not let anybody else do it** — the 1974 collection line, the Museum's own purple stamp struck through, *the series name is wrong and has been wrong since 1974 / nothing was disposed / this Museum paid no disposal fee and received nothing disposed*, and one line Mara Iles calls a liberty and Hattie keeps: *Wages were administered at the works by the pay office. The pay office kept its own book. That book is on the table.* **And when Elias points out that the collection line carries a worker's name, she says: *don't. Put the receipt on the panel and don't put a name under it. And on Friday you read that line out in that room, and if Perry Loach is in the room then he'll hear his own street, and if he isn't then the street's still been said. A receipt's a document. A name under a receipt is a saint.*** **The Museum's own plinth card stays in place underneath, because condition six says so.**

**The card, and the word.** The same card as October and as the seventh of November, five ruled lines, five things: **ONE QUESTION · NINE MINUTES · COST, NAMED BEFORE: ONE NAME. NOT MINE TO CHOOSE. · WITNESS WHO CAN STOP THE READER: M. ILES, REGISTRAR — WORD: NOT DISCLOSED TO THE READER · ABANDONABLE. IVO PELL MAY END IT AT ANY POINT BY READING THE BACK OF THIS CARD, WITHOUT ME, WITHOUT NAMING ME, WITHOUT ASKING ME.** And Ivo Pell's paragraph on the back, written at twenty to nine on the twenty-fourth of October in a loading bay for nobody. **And the cover on a trestle, and the clamp on the surround, and a man of twenty-five in the north door for two and a half months.**

**He asks for the word and Mara Iles refuses him in a conservation office with the door shut, and the reason is the best argument in the volume.** *If I tell you the word then you know the word. And if you know the word then at about six minutes in you will use it, because you will feel something and it will be the only thing in the room you can do about it, and you will use it, and it will be your word. And a practice that can be stopped by the man who is doing it has not been stopped. It has been interrupted.* And she hands him three lines and says the third is not hers, it is Ivo's, and she copied it onto a pad because she has a form for it and he has a plywood board.
