And then the abandonability, which is the whole of the chapter: **the nine minutes end at nine minutes whether it has answered or not, and after this morning he does not read anything again, ever — not the case, not the lamp, not a box in a cage, not a table in Ferry Bank, not a thing in a drawer in a laundrette — and the reason is not nobility, it is that as long as it is there it is the reason he gets in the rooms, and the rooms are the whole of what he does, and the only reason this building is in the state it is in is that it is full of competent people who have all said *that is not my department*, and I have spent six weeks learning that if one competent person moves about four inches to the left of her own department the whole building changes.** The question, twenty-nine words, in pencil on the back of the second page, written on a bench in a lobby: **whether the number that has been spoken in this room is the number of the people who came through the gate of the Sillick Mill on the morning of the thirtieth of October 1974, being the last working day.** It does not ask what the bell is for. **It does not ask about God, or a pattern, or a carried act, or a shrine, or a saint, or a name. It asks whether a number is true.** And under it the cost, with the seven people whose names he might lose, listed by category and not by name.

**Hattie Bramm's list of eighty is hers and is not given to the Museum**, done in pencil on the door, and if the number is exceeded she will tell them to go out. And the **three typed names** — three of them read back to herself off a list, and the fourth a woman who died in March — which she will hand to Field Officer Marsh in a room and not to the Museum, *because the second it is a thing that happened, one of the three is somebody's cousin.*

**And at ten past ten, after the count and after the shortfall and before the book is moved, the Director stands up against a wall with her hands behind her back and tells about seventy people, in about fifty seconds, without a document, what this Museum did in 1974.** Bought the office for no disposal fee from a firm of solicitors who had been asked by the mill's pay clerk to look at her own wages sixteen days earlier, and the letter was destroyed in 1979 under a schedule approved by a partner, and the only reason we know is that a man in that firm wrote a cross-reference in ink and initialled it in about nine minutes and did not have to. **And: we also accessioned, in the same year, a page that was not a mill document, on our own typewriter, four days after the purchase, and we called it disposed, and the word disposed is the sentence that has cost that book its own name for forty-eight years.** Then she sits down against the wall, and nobody thanks her, and it is the last hour of November in that hall, and **the thing about sixty people in that room remember, in the four weeks afterwards, in nine different rooms, is not the number and not the bell and not the book. It is a director, standing against a wall, at ten past ten in the morning, with her hands behind her back, saying we bought it for nothing, and sitting down afterwards.**

## Chapter 0046 — The Count, and One Note

**The memorial, and the batch's turn, and it is not a spectacle.** Hattie Bramm's letter of the twentieth is on the page and it is dated and postmarked and it names her as the person who will call the count, **and Elias Venn had nothing to do with it and nobody in the building had anything to do with it and that is provable on the face of a document in three different places.** Eighty people. A trestle six feet from the rope. The panel and the Museum's own card both on the plinth, and nobody turns the lower card over.

**10:08. The count is called, in a flat voice, with no performance, by the gate clerk, standing beside the trestle, and she does not use her own copy because that is going into a file and not into a room.** *The foreman called it and I wrote it and I read it back and he said yes. That was the count. That is what was called. It is not a number I have worked out. It is a number that was said out loud on a wet morning by a man to a woman with a book, in a place where every man in it could hear him say it, and no living person has said the whole of it since, and I am not going to say it twice, so somebody write it down.* **Ninety-four.** **And nobody in the room does the other thing, and the reason is not goodness: Field Officer Marsh ruled on Thursday evening that a difference is an adjudication, and about nineteen people in that room were over seventy and at least four had been on that gate, and in any other room in any other city the subtraction would have been done in four seconds.** About eleven people wrote it down.

**10:12 to 10:31. The shortfall is read out in full, name by name, out of the bound wage book, by the Registrar and not by the organiser, because condition five says no member of Museum staff states a figure — and Mara Iles ruled at nine that column headings of a pay-office book are figures about things and not about people.** For each line: a name, a grade, hours, a rate, a figure in the pay column. **And on about a third of the lines the pay column does not agree with the hours and the rate, and you do not have to be good at arithmetic to see it, because the book has it in columns set side by side and the pay column is the odd one out.** That is the shortfall, legible on the face of a page from 1974 without a single person doing a sum in their head. **Nobody said a total. The total was not read out, there is no numeral of it anywhere in this building's record, and the only copy of it left in the world is a sheet of thin carbon in a cage in a store in this city, and it opens at four o'clock this afternoon at a table in Ferry Bank with a gas fire on and a camera in it and no member of this Museum's staff in the room.**

**10:33. And then the forgery.** The Museum's own acquisition record on the table under condition nine, and in it the 1974 carbon receipt with its collection line — *P. LOACH (fitter), Sillick Lane, Low Ward* — and beside it, in the same typeface, in the same purple, on better paper, dated **the eleventh of November 1974, four days after the purchase, with no source line on it at all**: a clean typed page with a total at the foot, accessioned under a series called *disposed*. **Mara Iles reads the two dates, the two typeface lines and the absence of a source line out in two minutes, and says: *it is a forgery, it was made by this Museum, and I can prove that on the face of two pieces of paper and a date, and I have not been able to prove it out loud until today because until a few minutes ago it was in a box file behind a fire plan on the second floor of this building and nobody had ever read it in a room. I am not going to say who typed it. I do not know who typed it. The accession register of 1974 has a column for the person who presented the material, and the column for that item is empty, and it is empty in the register and not in my imagination.*** Then she puts it down and does not pick it up again, and it lies there in a plastic sleeve for about twenty minutes, and eleven people read it standing up, and a woman of about seventy picks it up and puts it down twice.

**10:35. THE DIRECTOR'S SPEECH, and it is here because it belongs here.** *This is where the batch's draft had it wrong twice and the review pass put it right: the speech was staged in Chapter 45 at 10:10, Chapter 46 did not contain it at all, and Chapter 45's own clock made it impossible because at 10:10 the shortfall had not been read and the book had not moved.* The Director stands up against the east wall with her hands behind her back and tells eighty people, in about fifty seconds, without a document, that **this Museum bought that mill office on the seventh of November 1974 for no disposal fee**, from a firm of solicitors who had been asked sixteen days earlier by the mill's pay clerk to look at her own wages; that the letter was destroyed in 1979 under a schedule approved by a partner of that firm; and that the only reason we know it existed is a cross-reference written in ink in 2024 by a man who did not have to. And that **we also accessioned a page that was not a mill document, on our own typewriter, four days after the purchase, and we called it *disposed*, and the word *disposed* is the sentence that has cost that book its own name for forty-eight years.** Nobody thanks her. Two people near the back are writing. Then: *I am being the Director of an institution that did the thing, and there are only two ways to be that: say so in a room, or have somebody else say it in a room with my minute attached.* She sits down and says nothing else for the rest of the hour.

**11:30. The one written line goes to a paper from a corridor**, after the room has let out, to a woman who has been standing outside since ten. Twenty-nine words, and she will not give it to a journalist herself. *This is where the press line lives now; Chapter 45 had it at nine in the morning quoting a meeting held the day before.*

**10:41. The handover is a document and a set of hands and nobody is given a plaque.** The book leaves the trestle, not accessioned and not to be, carried out through a public door by the person named at 1 on a form, into the custody of the conciliation service. **Hattie Bramm signs the receipt in ballpoint in about four seconds and puts her hand flat on the book for a second and takes it off, and nobody in the room read anything into that.**

**10:45 to 10:54. The reading.** His hand on the brass surround, below the case, where the earth lead runs, inside the reach of a clamp on the surround and not on the case. The case sealed, unopened, unreachable, the cover six feet away on a trestle, and nobody within nine feet of the plinth at any time between ten and eleven, and the two places where the floor is not level are taped. He does not read the card. **The pay office, the same one, the hatch, the slide down, the table with a lamp, the tin with a pay-office stamp, the blanket over the chair, and a book open at the last week in a hand that goes the other way, and a woman's voice saying a number once in a room where everybody could hear it — and it is the same number, and it was said the way a person says a number they have said before, which is a thing a reading cannot do, because a reading is a memory and a memory of a first time is a first time.** The date is on the corner of an envelope in a hard hand and it is the same date. **That is all he got. It confirmed the count, the record and the date and explained nothing, and the object did not appear and did not bless anybody and did not say who it was and did not forgive anything.**

**10:54. The cost, and one second later, one note.** The cost came first, and it is his own surname, and it took fifty-seven minutes. At about ten to eleven the Registrar's form wanted the name of the person who had done the reading and he could not produce it and she waited four seconds and did not fill it in and did not say *take your time* — **and a woman of fifty-four in the fourth row said it, and then said his name out loud, and told the room his name and Ivy Case's and that he was on the payroll in a building he is not inside.** Nobody in that hall knew; two people in that building did. Then the note: **one note out of a small bronze bell in a sealed case, not a crash and not a swell, arriving in a room rather than crossing it, four people put a hand on the person next to them, an assessor said afterwards that in her professional view it was a small object in a hard room being struck once by something and she would not be asked twice.** Then it stopped. **And it did not come again — not at half past eleven, not on the twenty-eighth, not on the twelfth, not on the thirteenth. There is no second note in the record of this building and the crack in its shoulder is still open under the staple and nobody in this volume was ever going to be able to make it heal.** Every term of the demand was met: a count of people, spoken in a public place, by a person with standing, entered in a record that will outlive the speaker. **Nobody was owed a miracle and nobody was granted a religion and nobody was given anything except a number they could now say, a book they did not have to pay for, a line in a file with a name against it, and a woman's standing in a room she had refused to stand in for nine years.**

**11:05, the front desk, and the cost made visible in a day book.** **He cannot sign out of his own building.** Margaret Priest, nineteen years at that counter, does not prompt him and does not help him and does not say *take your time*, and when he asks her to ask him a different question she thinks about it for a second and a half and then says *I can't do that one either, love, because I can't write the line without a name on it and I've got a rule about that* — and turns the day book round and offers to read it out, which she has never done for anybody in nineteen years. He cannot read it. So she reads the line out and writes under it: **11:06 — [LINE ENTERED BY COUNTER STAFF AT SUBJECT'S REQUEST.] … Name not spoken and not entered by subject. Counter staff cannot certify identity. Not to be queried. M. PRIEST, front desk.** *And then she said, to nobody, "That's the only time I have ever written that in that book."* He did not say his own name once between 10:54 and 11:52, and he said the count was on the page, and at the request of a woman of eighty-one he said *this has been about the letter* into a recording.

**11:10 to 12:20, the half landing below the fourth floor**, and at **11:52** he finds the name has come back by writing **12:04** at the top of the left-hand page and finding that *E. Venn* comes out of the end of the pen without a pause. He looks at it for about four seconds, and writes nothing else on that page that day.

**And at 12:04 the secondary latch on the unlisted door at the east end of the Quiet Wing corridor released itself, and the door moved about two inches and stopped, and nobody touched it and nobody opened it.** Mara Iles was at a table at the east end with the roll of the card-access record of the Hall of Former Faiths unrolled in front of her, because a Registry assessor asked for the **file** on the nineteenth of November and not for the roll, and because Anja Ward had put her on that level at half past ten that morning for a reason that was five fifths true. **She writes the time in the margin of a dot-matrix printout in about nine seconds, does not touch the door, does not get anybody, does not telephone the Director from that corridor, stands on the other side of the corridor from a door for about two minutes, and then leaves a message on a board that says *not reported as an incident* and *it is written in the margin of the access printout in my file*.** The dead civic repeater bracket halfway down that corridor has no bell on it and has had none for years, and there was nothing on it at twelve-oh-four and there was nothing on it at any time, and the repeater line the pressed-brass service bell is hard-piped to has been dead since the fifteenth of October. **Elias Venn was thirty feet below and around a corner, on a half landing, in a building he had been inside two days earlier and was not barred from until the following morning, and he learned the time at about twenty past twelve from a registrar, and he wrote it down, and the page is the fourth page from the end.** *The first version of this line said he had been barred from the building for four hours, which was false: the bar takes effect on 1 December and he is barred from it in the next chapter.*

## Chapter 0047 — What It Cost

**Everything the volume promised, in the order the outline fixes, and none of it is happy.** **The Hall is shut to the public from 11:00 on Friday the twenty-seventh under the minute, the shutters go on both doors at 16:10 on Saturday the twenty-eighth, and it reopens 09:50 on SUNDAY the thirteenth of December — sixteen days, and the outline's *a month* is not honoured and the ruling is in `state/continuity.md` R2**, and the closing card carries **Annexe D (permanent) is unaffected** because the Director made Ivo Pell put it on it, and a one-sentence Annexe C certificate that S. Spence read out loud to Mara Iles before she wrote it, which she has done since the twelfth of October and nobody has asked her to stop.

**The inquiry opens on TUESDAY the first of December and it is the first thing anybody in the building has asked for.** Its item 1 is the door and it is **reserved to the Director**, and item 1.2 is the only line in forty-one pages that three trustees agreed without anybody speaking: the sub-group does not need the room and does not ask to be given it. **And the Casket fiction breaks on the first afternoon, and it breaks on a phrase.** Mrs Vessey finds that **there is no damage record anywhere in the file of 18 October 2006 for the fourth level** — and there is a work order on the fourth level three weeks later, for a door, uncountersigned, marked not completed, closed by R.M.J. with *left as found*. **So the file of the structural accident is not a record of a structural accident; it is a record of a concealment.** The sub-group resolves that the file is preserved unaltered and not amended and that **a copy goes to the family of every person on the 2006 night roster, of whom there are four, and two have been traced in eleven days and one is alive and living in Low Ward and one is not.** And the Director is made to say, in the record, what she assumed for eighteen years — that the name on the instruction's face was a contractor's signatory — and that **it was never correct, and that the assumption did the job it was built to do, which was to stop a director from opening a file**, and that she built it on purpose in about nine seconds in a corridor in October and has not forgotten doing it.

**Dael Ordway is removed from night command by nine minutes of a minute, at 16:00 on the first of December, and it is correct and it is not a defeat and it is not a punishment and it is not the end of anything.** And the sentence that saves him is written by Mrs Vessey after four minutes of silence from the Director: **it is not a finding that the night supervisor falsified the entry in order to conceal anything, and he amended a line in a log in order to make a log shorter on the night itself, and a person who shortens a log on the night in order to be able to sleep is a person who should not be holding a night log at four in the morning.** And the minute records that he is a witness and **is asked nothing by the Director**, and records three documents already in the Director's possession since 24 November. He comes on the fourth of December with a plastic folder and gives them the log and the reason, and then gives them the true thing about Elias Venn, word for word, having said it first to a transfer clerk in a loading bay on the thirteenth of November and having never said it to him: **and I want the sub-group to have it because Mr Nunn carried it down two flights and gave it to him and the two of us have not discussed it and are not going to.** **Nobody in the room asked Dael Ordway a question about Elias Venn. There was not one, and the absence of a question is legible on a page.** And then he produces the two pieces of paper that save him, unasked, in his own working capacity, and asks for it to be minuted that way, and says: **I did not leave that line on the form because I am honest. I left it on the form because a form with the line on it is the only thing in this business that is going to keep me in a job, and I have known that since about half past one in the morning.**

**The families receive a documented claim and an interim payment, and it is not a settlement.** Nine pages, **CC/26/1104**, drawn on the Museum's own acquisition record, the certified comparison, the bound wage book and one sheet of carbon in a pay clerk's own hand. **£800 per named claimant, on account, not a settlement, and the letter says so four times** — and **it does not go to the people.** It goes into the conciliation service's account as **a schedule of interim payments which is a public document**, because *a sum of money handed to a person in a public room on a Saturday is a photograph, and a photograph is a receipt for the payer and not for the person paid.* **Fifty lines on the second of December and nobody knows how many there ought to be.** And the fifty-first line reads `51 — LOACH, P. — Sillick Lane, Low Ward. Fitter. Collected, 30 Oct 1974… Estate traced: none. Unclaimed. Held pending.` *The first version of this entry said fifty-one lines and gave him a house number, s.14, which is Hattie Bramm's and Cliff Sowerby's flat and is not on the 1974 receipt. Both corrected in the review pass.* — **nobody in this building wrote that line, and Elias Venn reads it twice and Mara Iles reads it over his shoulder and neither of them says anything at all.** In that hall three days earlier there had been a receipt on an easel with a collection line on it and a man in overalls forty feet away who did not move, and the panel said *a receipt's a document* and *a name under a receipt is a saint*.

**Wendel Case is restored on Saturday the fifth of December: a line in the staff register in a new hand, a leaver's card, an entry in an enquiry in the public record, a four-paragraph letter to Ivy Case at 4 Sillick Lane saying *we were wrong… there is no body and the Museum has looked and the family should not be asked to hope*, and his name back on the payroll line that should always have carried it, typed in by H. Lound at about eleven on the sixth with one and a half of two questions answered.** The findings behind it are four documents and there are no others: a 1994 bureau remittance at M.Q.S. 4 with deductions that have never stopped; a staff card in part illegible and in part amended, the amendment in a different hand and dated 2003; no leaver's record; and a minute in which a registrar read a name out because she had written it. **And Lound's second form, MPB/R/9016, with the null: *was this person employed by anybody? No response available from this office.*** And Elias Venn reads that form at a table in a laundrette at eleven at night on the fifth, out of a Jiffy bag with five documents in it and a covering slip that says *you are named on the fourth page of the item 4 minute*, and says **Case** out loud, forty days after four seconds in a loading bay and five weeks after fifty-seven minutes over his own name — **because his own came back at 11:52 on a half landing, and the object took one name on Friday and finished.** Nobody heard him. The register line carries **M. ILES and A. WARD and nobody else.**

**The envelope opens at 12 Ferry Bank at four o'clock on the Friday afternoon** — two conciliation officers, a supervisor, a camera, no Museum employee — and item 5 is one sheet folded in three, a carbon copy of a pay sheet, in a pay clerk's own hand, made at her own table in November 1974. **It agrees with the bound wage book, the hours column differs, and the weekly sheet was the pay clerk's working copy and the book was the firm's.** And there is no longer any corroboration in this city for either and there never will be, and **the final pay run now exists in exactly two forms — a bound book in the custody of a conciliation service in a hired room above a shop, and a sheet of carbon in a front room in Ferry Bank — and the shortfall is on the sheet, in total, in a pay clerk's figure, in her own hand, in 1974 money, and it is not in this volume and it is not in any Museum file and it will not be.** And it was never going to be proved by a bell.

**Cliff Sowerby is told at 14 Sillick Lane at about half past ten on the Saturday morning, by Hattie Bramm**, and the letter is **the Museum's own card, sent because the Hall of Former Faiths has a threshold group of about four hundred addresses and Low Ward is in it and nobody had ever sent him anything**. She took it in on the thirtieth of October and carried it in her coat and put it in a drawer with the hundred and forty *because I could not put a card about a bell in a drawer with a hundred and forty names because of what it would have been*, and she did not open it, *because it was not addressed to her and she is not a person who opens other people's post.* He reads it twice and asks one question: **is it the one off the poster?** And then: *when does it open again?* And Hattie Bramm reads the date off her telephone and says she'll take him on the tram, and *I'll put you on a list, because there is a list now, and it isn't mine, and there are four hundred names on the Hall's list and mine is a different one and the two of them are going to be in the same room in about a fortnight.*

**And the schedule of contents is rebuilt, or the finding is that it will not be finished**, in the shortest document in the volume: **INCOMPLETE. Expected to remain so.** Entries for 1974 cannot be completed until the sub-group has determined whether the series *Sillick Mill: administrative papers, disposed* was a disposal. **It was not a disposal and there is no evidence that any material in this collection was disposed of. The series is being renamed.** Eleven entries in March 2025, entries for 1974 marked *incomplete and expected to remain so*, **and the reason given: the entries require a decision about a series name and the decision is in an inquiry and an inquiry takes longer than a schedule. Recorded here so that no person in eleven weeks' time is surprised by an empty document and does not assume the worst.**

## Chapter 0048 — Four Lines

**The ending of a job, as a form, in four lines, and what the four lines do.** The minute of Wednesday the twenty-fifth, 09:25 to 09:55, present the Director and the registrar, minute taken. **One: the suspension is lifted, no finding of fault, work order 4412-S closed with no finding.** Two: **the appointment as night officer is terminated with effect from Monday the eighth of December under clause 11 of the Museum's Constitution, no fault being found and no right of appeal being conferred.** Three: **barred from the building as from the first of December as a security matter**, save attendance at any hearing or inquiry at which he is a party or a witness, on notice, in company, not before eight in the morning. Four: **no conduct complaint outstanding, no finding of misconduct, and the Museum has not investigated the conduct of any person.** Copy not to be given to the press. **Handed to him by hand at the front desk at half past eight on Friday the twenty-ninth, in a plastic sleeve, and the Director came down to the lobby to do it so that it was not handed over a counter, and said it was the last thing she was going to do for him in that building.**

**Line four is a gift and the worst document he has ever been handed.** In three weeks a letting agency in Low Ward will ask why he left and he will have to say *there was no fault*, and the woman will ask what he did instead, and he cannot answer, *because what he did instead was be the man in nine rooms, and every one of the nine rooms had somebody else in it who was better at it than he was and got the credit and the trouble.* And in four years somebody will be asked whether a suspended member of staff was on the fourth level at one in the morning in October 2006, and line four says the Museum has not investigated anybody's conduct, **and that is the correct answer and it is going to protect everybody including him and it is going to mean that nobody is ever found out, and he will have to decide in about eleven months whether that is a good thing, and he already thinks it is not, and he has no standing whatever to think it.**

**And the eleven minutes.** He gets four of them, on the page, because he wrote down afterwards that the number is the number. *You did give me a reason. In October. On a card, in your own hand, in a corridor, before you put it in a drawer. A person who causes the events he investigates should not be the investigator. And it is the only reservation you have ever held about anybody in this building, including yourself.* And: *that it's not me that's the problem. That it's the investigating.* He has spent six weeks finding out that everything he has is a procedure with a person in front of it, and in four weeks a woman in Northglass will decide whether a carried pattern may be displayed in a room, **and nobody in the world can do the thing he can do, and he cannot do it either any more, because he put it down on a card in a public gallery and said he doesn't read anything again, and a man of twenty-five in a doorway has a paragraph in his pocket that can stop him and he has never heard him say it.** And her answer: *that is right. And you are the only person in this city who worked that out in a fortnight and you are thirty-two, and I am not going to be the person who tells a thirty-two-year-old that he is right, because you are not — the working-out took a bell and a woman of seventy-eight and a man in overalls and a clerk on a quay, and none of those four people worked it out and all of them did the work.*

**And then the thing that is the volume's whole character in one scene.** Friday the twenty-ninth at twenty to nine at night, the key board: **Mrs Farrier, 58, fourteen years, who has never once in fourteen years asked anybody why, takes the card, writes 21:40 against the card number, and says *there's no leaver's card for you* — and then, without looking up, *there's not one for Case either. I don't know that from a file, love. I know it because I've been on this board fourteen years and the ones with leaver's cards have leaver's cards and the ones without don't, and there have been about nine in fourteen years and I could not tell you a single one of them what they were, and I have never once asked, because it isn't the board's job and I'm on the board.*** And he says it is the best thing anybody has said to him in six weeks, and she says *it's not a thing, it's a Tuesday*. And then: **because a suspended man is not a leaver, and a leaver's things are searched, nobody looked in his coat.** There is no form for it. He stood at a key board with a hundred and fifteen pages in the inside pocket and a day book open on the counter, and a woman of fifty-eight wrote a time against a card number and nobody looked, and nobody has ever known there was anything to look at, **and that is his punishment and it is his reward and they are the same object, and it is the single most accurate description of Elias Venn's entire six weeks that anybody in this book has produced, including him, and it was produced on a Friday night in December by a woman who does the key board.** The notebook line: *nobody looked in my coat… The Museum has not investigated the conduct of any person, and it is in line four, and it is the truth, and it is going to mean that in four years nobody knows what happened in this building in November and that is going to be the correct answer and I am going to have to be able to live inside it.*

**Mara Iles refuses him a reference and then gives him one**, and the four lines of it say what he did and not why, and she forbids him using *no finding of fault* in a covering letter, and tells him what to say to a letting agency (*I was a night officer in a public museum for three years and I was good at doors and clocks and locks and small repairs and I believed those were sufficient*) and to **not tell the next one about the note on the card**, and gives the reason that is the whole of the volume's method: *the moment a person in a kitchen in Low Ward knows that a bell in a museum can be asked a question, that person will go and ask it… and there is no form in this country that can hold a chain of eleven good intentions, and the only thing that has ever held anything in this business is a record with a name against it, and you cannot get a name against that.* And when he says he has been told not to do something five times this month, she says *keep it, and put it in the notebook, and don't show it to anybody. Item eight.*

**And the crate closes.** Hallowgate's reply of the first of December, three lines: paint, a length of conduit, one office chair, and a bundle of papers which was not listed and which they believe to be invoices; they hold no schedule; they are not able to assist further — with a 1974 in a 2024 sentence and an *[sic]* that nobody corrects. **And Elias Venn does not put it in a Museum file, on his own decision, under clause six, because a document that has been disclosed to a museum and then not asked for is the exact thing that form was built to stop.** It goes in a cardboard folder bought for one pound ten and labelled in his own hand with the reason, on a shelf between a boiler and a chair, and the last line is: *there is a chair in there, and the chair is a gate clerk's chair, and a woman of fifty-four sat in one of those for a working life, and the invoice bundle is in a company's compound and the company has no schedule and will not be helped, and I am not going to be the man who goes and asks a second time about a bundle of invoices in December.*

**And Doreen Sarn opened the Museum's registered letter on Sunday the sixth of December at about half past nine at night, alone, with the gas fire on, and read it for about eleven minutes and put it face down on the table again, and nobody knows what is in it, including Elias Venn, who carried it for nineteen days and did not read it and was told not to and did not ask to and did not ask afterwards.** And it is the last private thing in the volume that anybody is owed. **And the twenty-eight days expire on Thursday the seventeenth and there are eleven of them left, and nobody in the building knows that there are eleven of them left, and there is a woman of thirty-four in Northglass who wrote a date on the back of her own copy in pencil and would not send it.**

## Chapter 0049 — A Hundred and Fifteen Pages, and a Box for a Period

**Monday the eighth of December, the most tedious day of his working life.** He pays the pound and walks the ground floor and looks at the weighbridge poster for about four minutes, because he put it up, or had it put up, and cannot work out why he did it, and writes the only note in the notebook that is about a want: *I wanted a thing to be in a room that I put in a room, and I have not got one of those and I am not going to get one, and a poster at four foot six is the smallest version of it a man can commit and get away with.* And he reads **clause 11 of the Museum's Constitution**, borrowed from the Sillick Lane branch library: two lines, a Director may determine the terms of the employment of any officer, **no reason required and no appeal, because there is no finding to appeal against** — *the shortest and most complete document in this volume and it is four hundred years old and it is on the second page of a book in a public library and I had never read it.*

**And the notebook closes, on Friday the eleventh of December, in a conservation office, with the door shut, on a date he gave her on the twenty-third of November and she wrote down so that he could not move it.** A hundred and fourteen pages, unaltered, from a plastic wallet that cost thirty-five pence, in date order except for three pages he has not renumbered *because renumbering it is a thing I would do on my own in a room with a door shut and it is exactly the thing I am not doing.* **Three things before she opens it, and the second one is the only line in the volume in which Mara Iles says she is afraid.** One: she will read it there, he will sit there, she will ask no question while she does, and if she wants to know something she will write it on a pad and ask afterwards, and the answers will be typed and initialled and it will be a Museum record **and it will not be about you.** Two: **she will read the last page first** — the page he gave her on the twenty-third — *because a receipt is a thing you put in a drawer when you have decided to trust somebody, and I have not done that with a document since I was about nine.* Three, and it is the hard one, said before she starts: **I do not know what you are for. That is not a figure of speech and I am not being kind. In no case has the reading been the answer to a question, and in every case it has been a thing that happened in a room while a man was standing in it, and I have spent six weeks waiting for a form that says what the room is supposed to do. And there isn't one. And you are going to spend the next eleven months looking for somebody to be in a room with you, and there is not going to be one, and the not-going-to-be-one is a policy, and the policy is being written by a woman of thirty-four in Northglass who has never seen a piece of the evidence and has been right about everything.** He answers: *that's all right. That's the second time anybody's told me. The first was a woman of seventy-eight in a flat in Ferry Bank on the sixteenth of October… as long as it's there it's the reason I get in the rooms, and the rooms are the whole of what I do, and I have been the only man who can hear something in a public museum for six weeks and the only reason that has not destroyed this building is that the people I have been doing it to have been competent.* And she says that is the best sentence anybody has said to her in five years and reads the hundred and fourteen pages for twenty-two minutes without a question and without a fuss, turning four pages upside down once and reading a page of the first reading twice and the four seconds of a name nine times, and then: **that is a record and it is now a Museum record and it is about a Museum object and it is in my file and it is not about you, and you do not have it back. What you wanted was for me to know you. And I do know you, and I have known you since the sixth of October. And I would like you to understand that a hundred and fourteen pages is not a price. It is a record.**

**And on Saturday the twelfth at about twenty past eleven, five days before it expires, the form goes in — and the volume ends as a piece of A4 with a box on it for a period.** He wrote it out on Monday the seventh, in eleven minutes, and did not show it to anybody, and the reason is on the page and is not secrecy: *the reason is the box for a period, and the box for a period is the whole instrument, and I have spent two months learning that the box is the document.* **QUIET ACCORD — REQUESTER'S FURTHER QUESTION. Form A/3.** The question: **whether, before it certifies, re-certifies, or permits the continued display in a public room of any Schedule 12 carried object, the Registry will ask the question the schedule has no box for: how many people are in the room, and how old are they.** **Period sought: TWELVE MONTHS**, with the reason written in the space provided: *because every other period in this business has been about somebody needing something by a Friday, and this one is about every Friday after this one. The Registry's inspections are announced on a rota… I would like to be in the room for a year of them.* **It is the only number in the volume that is about the future rather than about what has already been counted.** Registered, acknowledgment of delivery, eleven stamps, £4.60, the only money he has spent in eleven weeks on anything. Then he stands in Lantern Row for four minutes and telephones Mara Iles, in ninety-one seconds, **and the telephone call is the ninth thing he has not told anybody about and the first one he told on purpose, and the reason he posted it first is on the page: if I know that I am going to tell her, I will not tell her, and then it is a thing I have done alone.** And her one thing back: *Roth will not send you anything back. She will list it, and it will come to a day with a name on it, and you will get a letter with a date and a reference on it, and it will be from a woman who has never seen a piece of the evidence, and she will be right, and it will take about eleven weeks. And in eleven weeks you will still be thirty-two in a profession that does not exist… and that is the whole of what a form is.* And she says *I am not going to say anything, and I am going to ring you back at eight o'clock on Monday because I am not doing this on a Friday, and it is Friday, and I have had a hard six weeks.*

**And the door is not opened here, and its hour is fixed.** Item 1.2 of the inquiry: the Director will open it herself, as the first act of the inquiry, **at 09:50 on Sunday 13 December**, at the same hour as the reopening of the Hall, and **no member of the sub-group, no member of Museum staff and no member of the public will be admitted to the fourth level for that purpose except the Director, the Registrar, the conservator, and one person who is not Museum staff and is not a member of the public.** The Board Secretary typed the clause on the Director's instruction and **the Director did not minute herself**, and she has not met him. **He has not been told. He is barred from the building and his appointment has ended and a man who is not Museum staff and is not a member of the public is nobody's responsibility on a Sunday morning at ten to ten, and the notice on the board outside the Hall has been taken down and replaced with one that says nothing at all, because there is nothing left to say about it on that board, and that board has been the most frightening document in this building for two months.** He will be told by post — the note goes into a box in Lantern Row at about four o'clock on **Saturday the twelfth** — and lands on **Tuesday the fifteenth**, on Museum notepaper with no number on it, and the volume ends before he reads it.

## Chapter 0050 — Sillick Lane

**The fixed final image, and it is the exact inverse of Chapter 41. REWRITTEN AT THE HEAD IN THE REVIEW PASS.** He is on the pavement from half past nine on **Sunday 13 December**, in a coat, with a pound he is not going to spend, **and the reason he is not going in is the bar: a piece of paper in a plastic sleeve in his coat, dated the twenty-fifth of November, saying he is barred from the building as from the first of December as a security matter and that there is no finding and nothing to appeal.** Nobody has told him the Hall is reopening; **the date came off a printed card in a newspaper, and there is no form in the world for a man who is barred from a building turning up outside it on the morning a room he had been inside thirty-nine days opens to the public.** *The first version of this chapter had Mara Iles's note arriving through a Low Ward letter box at half past two on the Friday afternoon and him acting on it, which is a document that cannot exist — it was posted on Saturday the twelfth at four o'clock and Chapter 49 spends its last four paragraphs establishing that he will not hear until the Monday. The note is still posted on the Saturday and he still reads it on the Tuesday. This is the only place in the volume where the review pass moved a load-bearing scene rather than a fact, and it was moved because Chapter 49 and Chapter 50 could not both be true and Chapter 49 is the one that carries the volume's last structural beat.* The lobby is open and the doors are propped and there is a card on a stand, and from the pavement at about twenty to ten he can read all of it — the Hall reopening today at 09:50, the object not handled, moved, opened or approached, the rope line unchanged, the panel reproduced without amendment, and the Museum's own card in place beneath it since 27 November. **And there is no Museum staff on the door**, which is true and which nobody arranged: the front-desk rota was cut to a skeleton for the term, a staffing notice went up on the eleventh with a line about the Gallery of Unnamed Hands being closed for a condition survey, and only the Board Secretary read it, and she read it on the Friday and went home. He can see the Hall from the pavement — the far end of the plinth row, the rope, the case at the east end, and **something on the front of the plinth that was not the plinth card.** About forty people in the room at ten, and among them, on a bench against the west wall with a folded newspaper on his knee, **Cliff Sowerby, who came on the tram with a woman of fifty-four and paid a pound each at a counter.**

**At ten minutes to ten, four floors above him, on a level he had not been allowed on since the nineteenth of October, the door was opened.** There was nothing about it on the pavement; he finds out on the Monday; and the sentence that carries it is that it is **the only gift in eleven weeks, and it was given to him by post, on a sheet of Museum notepaper with no number on it.** **At four minutes past eleven, in a hall he could see and could not touch, and about ninety feet from a man standing on a pavement in his coat, the bell gave one note. It was the same note.** About thirty of the forty people in there were there for a wage and not for a bell, eleven of them had been in that room on the twenty-seventh and had heard it, and **not one of the forty wrote anything down and none of them knew who to write it to — so the Museum's record of 13 December 2024 is a sheet of notepaper on a stand by the turnstiles, and the only document anywhere about the second note is a line in a notebook of a man who could not see the case.** That is the reconciliation with Chapter 46's *there is no second note in the record of this building* and it is the reason the record is silent. **The crack in its shoulder is still open under the staple.** **Elias Venn did not go in.** He stood there until a quarter past eleven with two hundred people going past him into a building he had been paid to stand in at night for three years, and then got the tram down the hill, **because the question had moved to where the people were, and a man who can only guard buildings has been told, in writing, in a plastic sleeve, that he is not a building.**

**And Volume 02's question is on a pavement corner at the bottom of Sillick Lane at about half past eleven on SUNDAY the thirteenth of December.** A postman of about fifty, a bundle of letters, a handheld, standing outside number 88 for about four minutes: **there is no 86 on the job sheet, and there never has been, and the field for 88's name is empty and has been since the sixth of December, and the depot says the sheet is right, and the sheet has never not been right in his life — a name field doesn't go empty by accident, somebody's typing.** And the same week: nine addresses off a road in Broad Street in three days. And Elias Venn writes four lines into the notebook, and the last one is: *This is not the mill and it is not 1974 and it is not a bell. It is a name field and it is empty and it is the same size as a person.* He does not touch the handheld, because it is a person's hand and a device, and because a man who has been the reason a device came out of a coat once in six weeks is not going to be the reason a screen gets looked at twice — and because the printed line on the day book four miles away is eight weeks old and he had read it that morning. And then he goes up the street past a woman of eighty-one at number six and a woman of fifty-four with a hundred and forty names in a drawer at number fourteen — **the woman he has been not asking a question for eleven weeks, who handed the only sentence in the case to a man of twenty-five in a gallery, and who said in a gallery on a Sunday afternoon in November that the ones she cannot get are the people who never had a letter, and that there is no list of those, and that there never will be, because you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept** — and he knocks on a door at number 88, at about ten to twelve, on a Sunday morning in December, **and it is a door he had never knocked on, and nobody in it had ever been asked for anything by anybody, and there was no form in the world that could help him, and there was no anchor card and there was no card and there were no nine minutes.**

**He knocked anyway.**

---

# VOLUME 02, CHAPTER SUMMARIES — BATCH 0001 (CHAPTERS 51–60)

## Chapter 0051 — The Fourth Day

**Tuesday the fifteenth of December 2024, 08:20 to about five in the afternoon, a rented room above a laundrette in Low Ward, a letting agency on Sillick Lane, and number 88.** It is the fourth day since a woman wrote his name on a piece of paper and put it through a letter box, and he has counted it four times without deciding to, and the title of the chapter is that count and it is not a good sign.

**The post.** Three things on the table: **the acknowledgment of delivery for Form A/3**, posted to Northglass on the twelfth with eleven stamps and £4.60 and signed for by a person whose job it is and whose name is not on the card; a letting agency leaflet about key-worker rates; and **a sheet of Museum notepaper with no number on it.** The note says *Sunday 13 December, 09:50… at the east end of the Quiet Wing corridor on level four, the Director opens the door. There is a clause for you. You are not Museum staff and you are not a member of the public. Come at half past nine. Bring the notebook. Do not bring it into the room. — M.I., 12 Dec 2024.* **The morning it names is two days gone. He is barred from the building. He works out in about nine seconds that the clause and the bar are incompatible by design — a person who is not Museum staff and is not a member of the public is, by the terms of the exception, a person with no lawful way in — and that Mara Iles wrote the sentence on Saturday afternoon knowing it, and that the reason he is finding out on a Tuesday is that the post comes on a Tuesday.**

**He has a pound.** He had two on the eighth and spent one walking the ground floor of his own building. This one is a different pound, because he bought a newspaper on Saturday morning for forty pence and did not eat it, and the newspaper is in a bin in a laundrette. He is carrying the same pound since Friday and it has not gone anywhere and neither has he.

**The agency, 09:15, four rooms above a shop that sells keys.** Sonia Boulton, thirty-nine, a book with a soft spine and a ribbon in it. *Current employer.* **None.** *Employer, then. Who's paying you.* **Municipal payroll bureau, Low Ward, cost centre M.Q.S. 4.** The biro stops for a second and a half and she says the true, competent, unkind thing: **"That's not an employer, love. That's a place the money goes through."** And then, better, and this is why she is the second person in this business to be good: **"Being on a list's not the same as having somebody ring you up. A list tells somebody what you did. It doesn't tell anybody what to do about you."** She reads the four-line reference twice. *This doesn't say why you left.* **No.** *Then whoever I ring is going to ask me why you left, and I'm going to have to say I don't know, and then they're going to say that's not an answer and it won't be their fault.* He gives her Mara Iles's answer, verbatim, in one sentence, and stops: **"I was a night officer in a public museum for three years and I was good at doors and clocks and locks and small repairs and I believed those were sufficient. And then stop."** She waits for the and-then. There isn't one. *"That's a clean answer. It'll tell you now, that's the answer that gets a person a start, because a person who says I was good at doors and then stops is a person who is telling you the truth about the size of it."* He is put on the **Thursday stair list**, cash, paid Friday, *not a job, it's the thing you put on the reference while you get a job.* He asks whether he is on any list. **He is.** *Well, that's a list.* **And it is the only good thing that happens to him that week and it is a tick in a book.** He also lets slip the eighteen-year-old letter on the shelf above the boiler and cannot work out afterwards whether she heard it.

**Number 88, ten past twelve.** He knocks again; the door comes off the latch four inches; the man introduces himself before letting him in — **Frank Dunning, and *that's the name, and I don't know what you want and I'm not saying come in*** — and lets him in anyway, because it is a draught off that door that has been coming in since about 1994. **On the table under the newel post there is a shoe, a key, a folded jumper, a plastic wallet, and nothing that is a document.** He looks at it for longer than he needs to and catches himself. *"You weren't going to ask it, so you can ask it now, and I'll tell you, and it'll be nothing, and then you'll go."* **"There's never been anything on it. I've never had a letter. I've had a bin bag and a bank."**

**The wrong document.** A bin bag behind the door, a third full, and on the top of the pile a **bank statement** — Counterpath, issued 2 December, four occurrences of the name, **correspondence address: 88 Sillick Lane**, a direct debit to an energy company taken on the second, **balance 74.20** — and rubber-stamped in the corner:

> **UNDELIVERABLE — ADDRESSEE —**
> **Sillick Lane — 8 Dec 24**

**And the dash is the whole of it.** A paper came out of a bank on the second with his name on it four times and his address on it once; it went into a box on the third; on the sixth the field a postman works from at 88 went empty; on the eighth the paper came back through a machine with five words and a dash where the sixth word would be. **The money went out on the second. The letter came back on the eighth. Both of those are correct.** The bank is not a villain, the post office is not a villain, and Frank Dunning has not done anything at all: *That's the machine saying it. That's not me. I go down on a Tuesday and I pay the money at the counter and the woman gives me a bit of paper and I put it in my pocket, and if I'm lucky the bit of paper's got a name on it and if I'm not it says a dash.* And the sentence the chapter is for: **"That's the bit that gets people. The money's gone out on the second and I don't know it, and it goes out on the second whether I know or not, and that's the bank working, and I'd not have the bank do it any other way. And the rest of it's a bin bag."**

**And he asks the question, because he had decided to and nobody had asked him to: "What do you want me to do."** *Nothing. There's nothing anybody can do.* And then the fourth line of the notebook and the only entry in this book any other person will ever read: ***"Nobody's asked me anything about it, and there's nobody to ask, and the last one that asked me anything was the fella from the clinic about my blood, and he's not coming back till March."*** **He waited for the answer. He did not have to wait in six weeks of not asking.**

**The kerb, twenty past twelve.** The postman is not there. Eleven letter boxes in the length he can see and two on the corner, and one of the two has a printed strip in a plastic holder with a name on it and the other has a printed strip in a plastic holder and **the strip is blank. Not worn. Not dirty. Not weathered. Blank, in the way a field is blank when nobody has typed into it.** He walks the row. 84, 88, 90, 80. **No 86, and there never has been**, and the street grew round a mill gate and has never been renumbered. Four notebook lines, and the last of them: *Nobody has taken anybody off anything. A field that was full has become empty and everything that reads it has been correct all along. That is the whole of it and I have got a pound and a cardboard folder and no instrument, and the only place in this city that prints a name on a card is four miles away and I am barred from it.* **He gets the tram up the hill and the decision he makes is not about the Museum and not about the door on the fourth floor: it is that there is exactly one address in this city where a person's name is held in a printed field on a sheet of paper in a building four hundred people walk through every day, and it is open until six, and nobody asked him to go.**

## Chapter 0052 — Two Hundred and Ten

**Wednesday the sixteenth of December 2024, 09:10 to about five in the afternoon, a delivery office on Nether Kiln Lane, and the arithmetic.**

He is the **fourth person that week** to ask about a delivery round, which is the first thing the woman at the counter says and it is the most important sentence anyone says to him all day. She tells him what she will and will not do and the reason she will not do more is on a printed A4 on the wall: **RELATIONSHIP TO ADDRESS** and **REASON FOR QUERY**, and neither of them says anything about a person. *If you have got the address and the postcode and you're on the round you'll have a handheld, and if you've got a handheld you don't need me.* And: **"You'd write *missing* or *not received* or *wrong name* and I'd look it up on a different screen and I'd tell you what the sheet says, and if it's a name that's come off I can tell you it's come off, and I can't tell you who took it off or why, because that's not in my system and I don't have a key to it and I'm not going to pretend to."**

**The walk sheet comes off the printer without being asked**, because there have been four of him in a week and the whole of the procedure is in a drawer next to the printer. `FISHER LANE & SILLICK — FLS-2 — WALK SHEET. Revised 02 Dec 24.` Nine columns, about two hundred and ten lines. **He reads it down twice and is embarrassed and does it anyway and writes down afterwards that he counted the lines twice and that this is what he is.** **Nine of the two hundred and ten have nothing in the NAME column, and four of the nine are 80, 84, 88 and 90 Sillick Lane.**

**The consequence, stated as procedure by a competent woman of twenty-seven who is not a villain and is not going to be one:** *What happens is the name goes. And once the name's gone the handheld stops asking for one, and the item can't be completed, and it comes back… That's not a rule anybody made for anybody. That's a handheld refusing to hand a postman an item it can't account for, and it is the best piece of design in this building and I would fight anybody who took it out.* And then: **"It doesn't ask whether the house has gone. It asks whether we've got somebody to give it to. Those are not the same and that is the entire problem and it has been the entire problem since about 1994 and I have never once been able to say it to anybody who wanted to hear it."** **Nobody wanted to hear it.**

**The change log.** Four lines per event, printed, with columns: SEQ · DATE · TIME · ROUND · ADDRESS · FIELD · WAS · NOW · REASON · OP. Broad Street, 26–28 November, three days, about four in the afternoon, OP-2201. 88 Sillick Lane, **06/12 03:41, marked *(batch)* — set on 05/12 at 16:22 by OP-2201**, with a note in a woman's initials explaining that the 03:41 was the nightly address-file refresh and not a keying. 84 and 90 on 10 December at 16:04. 80 on 11 December at 09:47, OP-2207. **FLS-2: nine since 26 November. BWD-1: nine. Two other roads: three. Twenty-one in three weeks.** The legend at the foot, and the whole batch in four printed words:

> **NCO — NO CURRENT OCCUPANT. Set by walk survey only. Consequence: record closed to delivery; occupier field not required; item returned or offered to neighbour.**

**So it takes a walk survey. Somebody has to have gone.** And an operator number is a person on the field operations contract, which is not this building's contract; the name is in the field operations system; the depot has a window with six fields and one of them is the number; and the name is released to the employer and not to the public, *and that is a data protection thing and it is a real thing and I am not going to have a go at the man who enforces it, because he is right and I would want him to do it to me.* And: *there are about two hundred and thirty of them and they press about eleven thousand keys a day, and I could not pick one out of that for you and neither could the field operations system if you asked it nicely.*

**And the arithmetic, which is the chapter.** About two hundred and ten addresses. Nine in a fortnight, and nine more on Broad Street in three days, and he does the division in about eleven seconds and gets about a third and then stops, because he knows what he has just done. **He has taken a third of a street off a piece of paper in his head and written a word for it and the word was a fraction and the fraction was of households.** Hattie Bramm called a number at a gate in the rain in 1974 and a conciliation officer ruled on the Thursday evening, out loud, that no figure is to be stated in the same breath as another figure from the same source and **no one is to state a difference, because a difference is an adjudication, and she did not adjudicate, and she said she would rule against it if it was offered.** And he took a fraction off a delivery sheet at a counter with nobody in the room who could rule against it. **That is the whole of what he is on the sixteenth of December: a man who takes things off lists.** And the half of it he is proud of, that the door is a *hook* and a hook does not know what is on it, and the only way to find out what is on a hook is to pick it up, and he is the only person in this business who can pick things up, and he put it down in a public gallery in front of eighty people on the twenty-seventh of November and said the rest of it as well.

**The instrument.** The change log is not a document anybody gives out and she says why: *That sheet goes to a business. It doesn't go home with anybody. If I put it in a bin bag in this street then in a fortnight there's a man at a hearing with a printer's copy of an extract of a field operations log and no idea where it came from, and that's not a piece of paper, that's a weapon with no name on it.* **So she tears a receipt off the roll, turns it over, and writes four lines in her own hand and gives it to him, because that is her own handwriting on her own paper and it is not the sheet, and if anybody asks she will say a man in a coat asked her for it and she said it out loud in a room with a printer in it.** And then: **"You've been the fourth one this week and you're the first one who's read the code legend. That's the whole of my week."** He tells her he is the sort of man who is all right. It is a lie, she does not check it, and neither does he.

## Chapter 0053 — The Queue

**Friday the eighteenth of December 2024, 09:10 to about two, and fifty-one minutes in a corridor.** A back room at the Sillick Lane branch library with a table, six chairs and a kettle, booked on Fridays for years by a woman of fifty-four with her own money and her own name. **It is not a meeting. There is no agenda and no minutes and no chair, and there is a sheet of A4 on the table with about twenty names on it in pencil, and it is not kept.** About twenty people, nine of them from that row on foot.

**He is not in the room and the reason he is not in the room is the chapter.** Hattie Bramm sees him across a public library at twenty past nine and says *No* before he has said anything: *You've got a face on you like the sixth of November. There is no such room in Low Ward a person can be in without a form, and I have been doing this in a back room with a kettle for nine years. You'll stand in the corridor, and I'll come out every twenty minutes and tell you what I'm saying, and if you put your head round that door once I will stop and turn round and I will be right, and I have been right about you in a room about nine times.* **He stands in a corridor for fifty-one minutes with a working radiator that does not work and a notice about a closed local studies room, and the chapter says the plain thing: there is no room in this city in which a decision is being made that he is entitled to be in.**

**What Hattie Bramm said in that room, given four times that morning to four different people, none of them writing down the same thing, which is how he knows it was not a speech.** Field Officer Marsh came at half past ten with a folder and did not open it, and said two things out loud first: *what is said in this room is said in a room, it is not a claim, it is not a statement, it is not a record, and it goes nowhere* — and *I write down what other people have already done, with the name of the person who did it on it. There is no version of me in this room today.* Vera Pilling came with her niece and sat by the radiator and said, at the end: **"Nobody's counted them."**

**The two sentences.**

> **One: nobody is doing this to anybody.** I have stood in this room nine years and I have never once seen anybody come and take anybody off anything. There's no man. There's no company. There's no letter with a name on it saying your address has changed. What there is, is a form somewhere that stops needing a name, and everything that reads it has carried on being right. And I want that said in a room with a form in it, because the second it's in a room with a form in it it's a thing that happened.
>
> **Two, and this is the one I have been trying to say since the Sunday. The post is the register. Everybody thinks the register is the council and the doctor's and the school's and it is all three of those and all three of them are fed off the same thing, and the same thing is a bag on a man's shoulder every morning, and I have watched that bag go up and down that street for thirty-one years and I have never once thought about it, and I have been the gate at a mill and I know exactly what a gate is for, and a gate is for a man to come through it and be counted.**

**Doreen Sarn was asked** — properly, by a woman with a form, with two witnesses, on a Tuesday in 1974 and again in a room in December. The refusal came back at four minutes past eleven in the mouth of a woman who had come down from Ferry Bank on a bus to deliver four words: *She says no.* Asked why: **that it would be the sixth thing she has refused and none of the five before it moved either.** Her reason is unchanged since October: she will not be a record again in a room with a form in it, and the six refusals have not moved and she is right about all six.

**The hundred and forty is in a drawer and is not produced, and the reason is given out loud twice**, and Elias Venn hears it through a door left open four inches for the kettle: *a list of people is not evidence until it is in a file, and the second it is in a file it can be used.* The three typed names went into Field Officer Marsh's file and not that room's, and she is right about that too. And the one she does not answer, for about nine seconds, which is the only nine seconds of this book he has counted: **there is no list of the ones who never had a letter, there never will be, you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept — and it is not a gap in a record, it is the shape of the thing, and the shape of the thing is the only evidence anybody will ever have.** And she tells him where he got the sentence from. *You've had that off Ivo Pell. You've had it off a man of twenty-five in a gallery and you gave it away and you've never asked me for it.*

**And at twenty past twelve in the corridor he asks her one question — the eleventh time, after eleven weeks — and waits for the answer, and it takes about four seconds.** *On the sheet. The round. **Is your name still in the field at number fourteen.*** **"Aye. It's got my name on it and it's had since about 1974 and I have never once wanted to read it."** *Why not.* **"Because I'm the one who writes them."**

**And then, standing by the office, she tells him the thing that is the best line in the chapter and is not a line at all, and he did not ask for it:** *You've been in about nine of them and you were in all nine because somebody else was in the room first, and there is not one of us in there today who would have let you in, and not one of us is wrong, and if you had come in here without a face on you I would have given you a chair and put you on the list, and the list is a piece of A4 and a pencil and it goes in a drawer, and I would have done it, and it would have been the best Thursday of my life.*

**The notebook entry, written out longhand in fifty minutes at a table in a rented room, closes on the moral floor and on the instrument, and it is the only place in the batch that states them both:** *The only list of that row is a Friday afternoon in a back room with a kettle in it, and the list is a piece of A4 and a pencil, and it is twenty names, and it is not kept, and it is the people who turn up.* And: *on the sixth of December the field at 88 went empty and Frank Dunning did not find out until a bank statement came back with a dash in a box on the eighth, and a bank statement is not the post.* And: *on the sixteenth of December a woman on a counter at Nether Kiln Lane wrote out four lines of a change log in her own hand and said out loud in a room with a printer in it where it came from, because a man in a coat asked her for it, and that is the only instrument anybody in three weeks has handed to anybody, and she did it because nobody asked her to and because I asked, and that is the whole of the four inches.*

## Chapter 0054 — A Door Is Not an Object

**Tuesday the twenty-second of December 2024, 10:10 to about half past six. A front desk, a public gallery, a service corridor, a corridor on the fourth floor.** He is inside the building lawfully, on a printed witness notice with a date and a time and a room and the word WITNESS in a box, in company with the Registrar, after eight, under the one exception line three of a four-line minute contains. **He has not been inside since the eighth of December. He is not going to the first floor and he is not going to be, because the card sends him to the fourth floor and a man does not go where a card does not send him.**

**Frank Dunning comes in at ten past ten and pays a pound and has the mat under his arm**, rolled badly, with the perished rubber backing stuck to the outside of the roll like a hat brim. Hattie Bramm sent him, and Margaret Priest has never been sent anybody before and says so. **The card is in a box on the shelf behind her in a bundle of about four hundred, and it takes ninety seconds to find the right one because they are in the order of a print run.**

> **MUSEUM OF QUIET SAINTS — HALL OF FORMER FAITHS — THRESHOLD GROUP.** *Our records show this address as part of the Hall of Former Faiths threshold group. If that is wrong, tell us. If you would like to be taken off it, tell us. If you would like a copy of anything we hold about the object at plinth 34, tell us and we will send it.* **Annual acknowledgement. Issued 12 December 2024.** **DUNNING, F. — 88 SILLICK LANE, LOW WARD.**

**"It came back,"** says Margaret Priest, and then stops, because she has said more than she meant to, and a woman who has taken messages at a counter for nineteen years knows exactly what she has just done, and she does not take it back. It came back in the second week of December, all nine of that run together, and there is another lot in the box behind the boiler on Friday and nobody has opened the box on Friday. And she gives him the two things: **the comfortable one** (*it's a postal thing, there's a form at the post office and it'll come back*) **and the other one**: *We don't make that list. We print off a file that comes down from the district on the first of the month and we do not make it and we have never made it, and I have been on this counter for nineteen years and the names on that card came into this building off a computer somewhere and I have never seen the computer and I have never asked, because for nineteen years the only thing anybody has ever wanted from me is the right piece of paper and I have been extremely good at the right piece of paper.*

**The mat goes into the Hall of Former Faiths at twenty to eleven and is not accessioned and is not on a plinth and is not behind glass and does not go under the rope, and no card is made for it, and nobody reads anything.** The reason it goes into a public room at all is a sentence in a corridor on 24 November that a director cannot take custody of a thing she has no title to, cannot indemnify and cannot undertake anything about — and the registrar worked out in about four minutes that the only thing this building can do with an object it is not allowed to hold is **write down what it is**, in a room, where a person can see it happen. *There is no form in this building for a thing that is given to us by a member of the public in a lobby… and if I put it in the disclosure file then a mat is a document and in five years somebody will have built a category on the strength of my Tuesday morning.* And then Frank Dunning asks the one question that matters: **"Right. Can somebody write down what it is, then."** *Yes. That is the one thing I am allowed to do… and you are going to have to decide whether that is good or the worst thing you have ever been handed.*

**The description, four minutes and eleven seconds, with a steel rule, on Museum notepaper, in the presence of the presenter, with Elias Venn standing six feet away on the terrazzo with his hands behind his back — the hardest thing he has done since the twenty-seventh of November, and nobody in that building will ever know it was hard.**

> **DOOR MAT (as described). Coir on rubber backing, 700 × 400 mm. Wear: one band approx. 380 mm wide, centreline 120 mm from the edge nearest the door. The opposite edge is sound. The wear is therefore on the side of the mat that faces into the dwelling. Backing perished at two corners, one corner re-laid; one edge repaired, different fibre, two staples. No inscription, no name, no number, no maker's mark, no trade mark, no date. No evidence of any use other than standing on. Function: a fixed-size surface of the dimensions required for a person to stand on while a person stands on it, at a doorway, while a person decides whether to open a door. The wear records the position of the standing. No accession number assigned and none proposed. Not accessioned, not displayed, not labelled, not to be catalogued.**

**And the sentence the morning is for, said to Frank Dunning and not to Elias Venn: "It is seven hundred by four hundred. That is the size of a doorway with a person standing in it. Nobody chose that size. It is a size because it has to be one. And the wear is where they wait, and the wear is on the side that faces into the house, and that means the person doing the standing is the person being let in — and that is not a mystery and it is not a god and it is the oldest piece of engineering in this building and there is not one word of it on a plinth in this room."**

**And she will not make a card — and she gives both reasons, and neither is a rule.** *I am not going to put a name on a mat in a public room, and I am not going to put a blank on it either, and both of those are decisions and neither of them is a rule and I am making both of them and I would like them in the minute if anybody is writing one.* **Why not a blank?** *Because a blank on a plinth card in this room is a thing I have seen before, and it is on a card about eleven feet from where we are standing, and I have known about it since the sixth of October, and the reason I am not going to put a blank on a card about a mat in a public room is that I do not yet know what this building does with a card when it cannot be bothered to fill it in, and I have watched this building for five years and the answer is that it fills it in later.*

**And the rhyme, eleven feet away, which nobody has ever put in the same sentence as anything.** Plinth eleven: **1961/LW/19 — threshold stone, sandstone, one sill fragment. One hollow, worn, foot-shaped, right of centre. Recovered on the demolition of a mill building, Low Ward. No maker identified.** Nine inches by four. The size of a foot. **And the maker's line is empty and has been empty since the card was cut, and the empty is not an oversight.** *It is a threshold object. It is the oldest thing in this room… and nobody has ever been on this side of the row at eleven o'clock on a Tuesday, and that is not a criticism of anybody in this building, that is a hundred and forty years of a rope line and about four people a year who come to a public gallery and look at the plinths from the middle of the room.*

**And the sentence the next eight days are built on, said to a man in a cardigan holding a rolled mat: "Mr Dunning, you have asked me three times in eleven minutes to write down a thing about you. I have written down a thing about a mat. There is no form in this country that will put those two sentences in the same file, and the reason there is not, is that the sentence about you is about a person and the sentence about the mat is about an object, and this building has been very good for a hundred and forty years at the second of those and has never once been asked for the first."**

**Then the instruments do not reach it, and the reason is four sentences long and the whole of the volume is in them.** Conditions 1 and 2 apply only to Schedule 12 carried objects. This is not one. It has no classification, no number, no location in a systems index and no owner of record. **So there is no docket to be raised in or out, because a docket is a document about a Museum object, and a mat in a corridor is not a Museum object and cannot be made into one by anybody in the building without a form that does not exist.** It is on the floor of a service corridor, on a rubber-backed mat, on concrete, with a folding chair four feet away and a mop, in a week when about a hundred and fifty people a day come through the front door, and the Registry's rota is a paper on a wall in Northglass and the next slot is the sixth of February.

**And on the next day, Wednesday the twenty-third, at about twenty past two in the afternoon, a registrar drafts a movement docket anyway, because that is what she is — and it fails.** Item, quantity, carriage, destination, date, time out, carrier, and a countersignature in the space for **a person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum.** And then condition two again, and the sentence in the Director's own hand: *My signature is not sufficient.* **"The countersignature has to be somebody who is not an officer or employee. The only person who can sign for a mat is the man it belongs to, and he is a member of the public, and he would sign it this afternoon. And the moment the docket exists, condition two is engaged, and condition two says a Schedule 12 object is not to be removed except on a docket so countersigned — and it is not a Schedule 12 object, and the moment we make it one, in order to get it out of the building, we have built ourselves a rule that says he cannot have it back."** **It is the first time in this case that the best control in this building stopped a good person doing the right thing, and it is the first time the instrument that caught everything in Volume 01 is found to have no subject at all.** Mara Iles says it to a man on the stairs: *I want it noted somewhere that the reason a door mat cannot leave a public museum in this city is that there is no form for a person to write a person's name on when they are giving you a thing they own.* The last four words of the notebook entry for the twenty-second of December are: **A description is the only thing left.**

## Chapter 0055 — The Carrying
