That is the whole of my reason and it is not a moral and I am not being brave — I would do it gladly if there was nobody in that flat.** The Registrar says the cost out loud on a pavement and then says the thing: the card will carry the name and, under it, that it is unsupported beyond a district's retained prior value and that a person who knew the man declined to be recorded — **and the reason is not on it, because the reason is hers and because a reason on a card is a handle** — and it will be wrong on purpose, **and it is the best I can do with a no.** **Elias Venn did not write the name down. He has known it since 4 February and has carried a man's name in his head for eleven weeks and said nothing, and he writes that down instead.**

## Chapter 0088 — Where Has To Be (Fri 16 Apr 2025, 14:00 – 16:30, Morningside)

**A room booked on a form on Wednesday the fourteenth with a purpose on it — *to establish whether a practice requires a location* — and no minute, and four people.** Ivo Pell asks his question of 20 February properly, in three parts, having written it out and not reading from it: **where does a practice have to happen for it to be the same practice; what is the instrument that holds it there; and who is the person who says.** A letting agent answers two parts in about nineteen minutes and refuses the third: **a practice with a shop in it is portable — you can buy the thing it happens on for two pounds, and about nine of them have been sold since the fourth of January, and nobody's job is to know it. A practice with a landlord in it is not, and that is not a moral, it is a fact about what a practice needs. And there is no version of that answer where it comes from me, because the two answers are both true of my client and I do not get to choose which one you use — and if you want it answered then it has to be answered by whoever is going to be wrong about one half of it, and there is exactly one person in this room like that and she knows it.** And the Registrar puts the object-or-place question in one sentence, for the first time in nineteen weeks, in a room with a letting agent in it — and then **refuses it, with a new reason, in about nine seconds: *I am the person who will have to write it down*.** If she answers, then in about four years there is a document in this building with a registrar's initials on it saying what the practice is, in one word, in a public register, and somebody will be able to point at it and say *the museum decided* — **and the museum will not have decided anything; a registrar will have written down the answer to a question she was asked, in a room, on a Friday, and that is exactly the same mechanism as a card, and I have been on both sides of it since November.** Then the answer to parts one and two, which Mara Iles has had since 20 February and did not say out loud until this room, because she did not want to be the woman who tells a man of sixty-five that his hall is not a place: **there is no instrument and there is not supposed to be one. A practice is a permission. Any person may stop at any point and nobody is asked afterwards why. A permission cannot be located — you cannot put a permission in a place and keep it there, because a place holds things and a permission is a thing you are allowed to stop doing, and the person who is allowed to stop is the person who holds it, and they are on a step, not in a building. There is no instrument because a permission is not the kind of thing instruments are for, and there is not going to be one, and there ought not to be one, and I know every instrument there is and not one of them can hold a promise to say nothing.** And the third part: **nobody. There is nobody. And that is the correct answer.** The room produces **four lines of free text on a booking form and no minute at all.** And afterwards: two mats, one of them bought on the Thursday for two pounds by four people who put a pound each into an envelope, **because there is no form for a two-pound mat and nobody was going to put one on a minute** — and at about half past four on a street that is not on a sheet, a mat went down against a wall by somebody who has stood on two of them, and nobody recorded it and nobody has told the man with the table.

## Chapter 0089 — Checked By More Than One Person (Fri 23 Apr 2025, 09:40 – 13:20, the label room)

**A dome camera over a label-room doorway, a card cut to put over the lens with a date on it, no exposure made, and no countersignature required because nothing was made — and Ivo Pell's condition of the first of December working exactly as a man of twenty-five wrote it for a reason none of them could have given in November.** Four people and a records officer of about fifty who has been asked for one thing in six years and has not been thanked, who came in her own time and **refused a bus ticket twice**, and who says the difference between checking whether you kept what you were supposed to keep and checking whether what you kept was right is about eleven years of her working life and she did not know that until she was asked. **Mara Iles does not read the notebook, and the reason is the best decision anybody in this case has made since November and she made it about herself: everything she reads becomes a document in a museum file the moment it is read, and the minute of 27 January says this Museum holds no record of what happens on that street.** He reads all nine pages aloud, in order, for an hour and ten minutes. She brings two things: her own record of issue, nine entries in six years, and a diary and a wall calendar. **Four entries carried a date checkable against a document. Three were correct. One carried a day of the week that was wrong** — `Tue` against the eighteenth of March 2025, which is a Thursday, copied off a firm of solicitors' letter of 19 February that had the date right and the weekday wrong. **And she says it in about eleven seconds and nobody in the room recognises that anything has happened: a date is a fact about the world. A weekday is a fact about whoever typed it. There is no arithmetic in a weekday, and I have never once in six years checked a weekday against a source, and neither has anybody in this room, and I do not know one person in this city who has.** Then, to him and not to the room, in about nine seconds: **if I can be wrong about a day of the week off a wall calendar, then anybody can be wrong about a day of the week in a notebook, on purpose or by accident, and the only way anybody finds out is if a second person reads it — and I would like it noted that nobody thought to ask me for twenty weeks, and it was not because anybody was unkind to me. It is because there is no form for *is there a second person*.** A conciliation service reissues the letter on Wednesday the twenty-eighth with the weekday corrected and a line on its face saying what was wrong, and **the wrong one is not destroyed and the right one is on top of it and both are dated — the first correction at source and versioned in this case, and it is a day of the week, and it is the first of a kind and not the kind that matters.** A partner in Irving Quay telephones at twenty past twelve and does not apologise, and will write into his next letter that a weekday is evidence and that anybody relying on his correspondence should check them, and says **he would not have written that sentence in February.** And a card is cut in four minutes and put on a front desk in a public lobby that names no person and says that a day of the week in a notebook was wrong, and how many entries could be checked, and that the error was found because a second person was asked and not because anybody thought of looking, and **there is no form for a second person, and it is recorded here so that the next person who has a notebook reads this card first.**

## Chapter 0090 — A Boundary With A Date On The End Of It (Sat 24 – Sun 25 Apr 2025)

**A room above a shop, let by the hour, four pound notes and a pound coin in an envelope from about nine people, a key, and nobody's name on the booking — and the shopkeeper took the envelope, gave over the key, and has never been told what the room is for and has not asked.** The day starts at ten with about eleven people and a kettle somebody brought and no Museum and no document and no minute and no form. **At about four o'clock a man of fifty-two, who has a first-floor flat on that street and has had it for about twenty-six years, who has never been on a sheet, who has not been asked a question in three months, and who has watched about nine houses on that street empty in one December, says *I'll write it*, and gives the reason before anybody asks: nobody asked me, that's why I'm going to write it.** He drafts it in about four minutes with a bolt pen out of a woman's bag, on the back of a piece of paper that had been a list of shopping, and nobody reads it while he is writing it. **The first three lines are the rules that have been on a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet since the fourth of January, and the third has been there since the eighth in a different hand, and nobody in the room mentions that and nobody mentions it afterwards and that is correct.** Then: **it goes on until the first of October. On the first of October we stop, and then we will decide whether to start again. Nobody has to promise us anything and we are not asking anybody to.** Because the first of October is the day the programme is finished in this ward — **a thing with a landlord in it is a document with a date on it, and a thing with a cycle in it is a document with a date on it, and a thing with a date on the end of it that somebody chose is not a document anybody can enforce, and it is the first one anybody in this room has been offered, and it is the only one we are ever going to get.** Then the fourth rule, said by the man with the table before anybody asked him, because he has been waiting since 28 February for somebody to make him: **the fourth rule is *somebody is keeping it*, and I am not going to be the one, and I would have said yes on the fourth of January if anybody had asked and I am not going to be asked now, and a man of sixty-five who keeps a table in a hall is a man with a table in a hall, and the day I stop it stops, and that is the correct arrangement and it is the worst arrangement in the world in that order.** And then, in the last twenty seconds of the four minutes, the drafter reads it back and changes his mind, and the reason is nine words: **nobody asked me.** He has been in the room since ten o'clock, he says, and he has been the only person in it that nobody asked, and both that and not asking are the same mistake and one of them is his. **Nobody is asked to sign anything on the Saturday or the Sunday, and the fifth line on the wall says that nobody signs it and nobody keeps it and it is on the wall.** And then somebody asks about a register, and a man of fifty-two refuses it in about nine seconds, and his reason is new: **a list of people is a list of people and I agree with that. A list of houses is worse, and nobody has said that. There are about nine empty flats on this street and there is nothing anywhere in this city that says what it was — not the district, not the delivery office, not the Museum, not the hundred and forty in a drawer, not a letting agent's book, not the practice sheet.** Sunday morning: the key back, five pounds, the shopkeeper shuts the door and has never asked, and about six people stand on a mat from three o'clock until you cannot read a number off a door, and nobody keeps a list. And four miles away a bell under a panel a hundred and nineteen days old **gives no note**, and has given none for one hundred and thirty-three days, and **no bell is on a step and nobody has ever asked the two of them about each other and nobody is going to.** The last image is six people owed nothing and four refusals in three weeks: **I was the only person in this case who had nothing on the sixth of December. I am not now.** And the last line is the question, and it is louder than it was on the sixth of February: **who keeps it.**
